FAITH. JUSTIFICATION. Show wherein [consists] the grand and important difference between the orthodox faith concerning justification, and the opinion of Arminians and all neonomians and all opinions holding any legal way of justification. Not that our being justified depends on something that is in us, or something that we do; and so, in Scripture language, "some work of ours" (for faith is so). Not that something that we do, or some act or work of ours, properly interests us or is the proper condition of an interest in Christ. But it is the manner of its interesting us. It is [not] whether faith, or any other acts or works of ours, interests us as our good works, or by virtue of the goodness and amiableness of the act or work; but whether it be only by virtue of the relation it bears to Christ as a proper receiving of him. He that is orthodox holds that our holy practice interests us only this way, viz. as it is an expression of our receiving Christ. The others hold that faith justifies only by virtue of its loveliness. One holds that our holy works justify only as expressions of faith; the other holds that faith justifies only as an holy work. The difference is not whether works, with respect to anything in them, do justify: for all that hold that [we are] justified by faith, hold that a work with respect to something that is in it does justify, because faith is a work. And 'tis no more legal or against the free grace of God to suppose that many works justify than one; but that one work does justify as a good work. No more does holy practice. Mention this in the stating of the question. Show that the difference is vastly great. Show particularly in what respects the holy Scripture and orthodox divines seek to magnify free grace in the doctrine of justification, not being merited by benefiting God, or by goodness and amiableness, to balance by amiableness, to attract, etc. These are the ways, and all the ways, that can be devised. Inasmuch as faith is a work or a good act, it hence follows that the thing that is contrary to free grace in the doctrine of justification by works, is not that something that is indeed a good work of ours should be the special condition of justification. Show first in the beginning what is meant by a work in Scripture language; and here observe that it is any virtue or good act of ours. Thoroughly to improve that argument of the inconsistency of Arminian principles with our prayers and thanksgivings to God, show it under the several heads. EFFICACIOUS GRACE. Remember to consult the Lime-Street Sermons.[^005-note188] Remember to borrow Mr. Locke's *Works*, his *Reasonableness of Christianity* and annotations, to see his notion of justification by faith and not by the works of the law, and particularly consider and confute it.[^005-note189] To confute Dr. Watts' notion in the Berry Street Sermons, sermon 13, that by "the law that could not give life" that the Apostle speaks of in Romans and Galatians is [meant] the Sinai covenant or temporal covenant made with the Jewish [people], whose precepts were the Ten Commandments with the ceremonial and political law, and promises only [the] temporal blessing of Canaan; which, being considered apart from the gospel, was a covenant of works.[^005-note191] It were to be wished that persons that light on new notions of this nature, before they vent 'em and publish 'em to the world, would consider 'em more fully in their length and breadth, and make things out more fully and clearly in their own minds, and not trust to a little, obscure, faint but pleasing plausibility on a new-invented scheme. 'Tis strange how the words of this Apostle have been twisted, wrested and wreaked[^005-note192] to avoid the true, proper, plain and only possible meaning of them. Answer the objection from Hebrews 8:6, "A better covenant, established on better promises," a better dispensation. That dispensation chiefly dwelt on externals; multitudes of external carnal ordinances and other externals of religion, and the external rewards and punishments, were chiefly insisted on in that revelation. And these things insisted on were in a legal manner. But now chiefly the internals and spirituals in this revelation, spiritual and eternal promises. External things, or carnal things and legal things, were mostly exhibited and most visible under that dispensation. Those things by which the child was treated as a servant were most exhibited and most visible and most used. Moses, a temporal and typical mediator, was most visible. Now we have a better mediator, made chiefly visible; better conditions or terms; better promises; a better sanctuary; a better priest, better sacrifices, better Holy of Holies; a better tabernacle and temple; a better Jerusalem; a better mountain, not Sinai but Sion; better tables of the Law, not stone but the heart. To show that by the works of the law, which the Apostle says we are not justified by, is not meant only the outward obedience to the law. Answer that objection, that that place seems to favor it, as "touching the righteousness which is in the law, blameless" [^005-note193]; i.e. as the Pharisees and the Jews in general from [old] understood the law. As in the foregoing verse, the Apostle says,[^005-note194] "touching the law, a Pharisee"; i.e. he interpreted the law in the manner that the Pharisees did. And now he adds that, according to that interpretation, he was blameless touching the righteousness of the law. He can't mean otherwise, for elsewhere [he] observes that the law, rightly understood, condemns all as it says, "Cursed is every one that continueth not in all things which are written in the book of the law to do them" [^005-note195]; and many other things of the like nature. Induced by legal motives and principles and a regard to the temporal promises and threatenings. That by works is not meant merely the merit of good works, if they mean by "merit" that merit that can be a foundation of a demand. ORIGINAL SIN. If those that die in infancy, not ingrafted into Christ, have no positive eternal punishment, then they are either annihilated or are in a state of indifferency. I would ask those that suppose they are in a state of indifferency, how they live as to sin and holiness: whether they live a wicked or holy life in another world? If a sensual life, and so are left to go on eternally in sin, doubtless they live a miserable life. And how can it be otherwise, if their being be continued and they die under the fall of Adam: for why should not the consequence of the fall, as in another world, be sin, a sinful heart and life, as well as in this world? FREE WILL.[^005-note196] If by FATE they mean the preceding certain futurity of events, and the certain unfailing connection of future events with something that already has being, I acknowledge such a fate as this, and so do they too: they own the divine foreknowledge, and that that is a thing that already exists, and that there is a certain futurity of the events foreknown, and a certain connection of those events with the foreknowledge that already exists. If this preceding certain futurition of events and their certain connection with present and past existence be what they mean by fate, it alters not the case whether that connection be with one present or past thing, or a thousand, as long as the futurity is as certain as the connection is[^005-note197] sure FREE WILL. Take notice what things the Scripture represents to be sins, yea, damnable sins, under the head of persons' being not excused because of a moral inability. And then observe how differently the Scripture representations of things are from the representations of this wise age. PREDESTINATION. See the last part of *Thesaurus Biblicus*, entitled *The Bible's Abstract or Epitome*, pp. 17-22.[^005-note198] These[^005-note199] arguments may be turned off with this: "This is nothing but a METAPHYSICAL DISTINCTION; away with these metaphysical subtilties"--as many other good and solid arguments have been, that could not be answered any other way. The question [is], whether they are distinctions founded on truth and good reason. If they are, 'tis not the epithet *metaphysical* (nor any other sounds or letters you may signify 'em by) that will destroy 'em. FREE WILL. SELF-DETERMINING POWER. *Corol*. Seeing it is so that this necessity or certainty of the acts of will not being at all inconsistent with our freedom and moral agency, and our being properly the subject of commands and rewards and punishments with respect to these acts of [will]: hence it follows that our freedom, moral agency, etc. are not inconsistent with the absolute decrees of God determining these acts of will and with absolute election, etc. Another *corollary*, showing that as it is not inconsistent with commands, so not with offers, calls and invitations. And therefore, that the absolute decrees of God are not inconsistent with such offers, calls, etc. [^005-note200] JUSTIFICATION by their own righteousness, Dr. Doddridge supposes, is meant a compound of morality, consisting in abstaining from the grosser violations of the moral precepts together with sacrifices and ceremonial observances, to atone for lesser breaches (in note on Mark 10:20). To show that this is not all that the Apostle means by our own righteousness, when he asserts that we are not justified by our own righteousness, but[^005-note201] "the righteousness which is of God by faith" [^005-note202], that more thorough obedience to the law in its spiritual meaning, exceeding the righteousness of the Scribes and Pharisees; which faith is the principle of, as Dr. Doddridge seems to suppose in the same place, speaking of the righteousness which the young man in the Gospel trusted to.[^005-note203] ELECTION. Particular redemption, special vocation and perseverance: see Mrs. Dutton's *Letter to Mr. John Wesley*.[^005-note204] According[^005-note205] to the usual meaning of the word POWER, if persons have a thing in their choice, they have it in their power. What God ;scPERMITS;rt, he DECREES to permit. If it is no blemish to God to permit sin, then it is no blemish to him to purpose or intend to permit it. And if he [be] omniscient and does at all designedly permit that sin that actually comes to pass, then he designedly permits that sin that, if he, knowing if he permits it, will actually come to pass. And this is an effectual permission, and all that we plead for: and what then do our adversaries quarrel with us for, and pretend that we charge God with being the author of sin? Christ drew things from Scripture by CONSEQUENCE in his argument with the Pharisees from that text, "I am the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob. God is not the God of the dead, but of the living" [^005-note206]. UNIVERSAL REDEMPTION. In some sense, redemption is universal of all mankind: all mankind now have an opportunity to be saved otherwise than they would have had if Christ had not died. A door of mercy is in some sort opened for them. This is one benefit actually consequent on Christ's death; but the benefits that are actually consequent on Christ's death and are obtained by Christ's death, doubtless Christ intended to obtain by his death. It was one thing he aimed at by his death; or which is the same thing, he died to obtain it, as it was one end of his death. There is a way of drawing CONSEQUENCES from Scripture that begs the question. As the Arminians say, there are many more texts plainly against election than seem for it, viz. those texts that represent as though general offers of salvation are made and as though it was left to men's choice whether they will be saved or no. But that is begging the question, for the question very much consists in these things: whether an absolute decree be inconsistent with men's liberty, and so with a general offer of salvation, etc. IMPUTATION OF ADAM'S SIN.[^005-note207] *Obj*. When God made the covenant with Adam, he did not mean thou and thy posterity shalt die, for he says, "In the day thou eatest thou shalt die" [^005-note208]; which implies that he should have no posterity. DEATH threatened to Adam. *Obj*. That expression, "Thou shalt die the death," often used by Moses, signifies only temporal death. See Genesis 16:2, Exodus 2:12, Exodus 2:15-17, Exodus 2:20; Leviticus 2:9-11, 13, 15; 1 Samuel 14:24. Whitby, Preface.[^005-note209] DECREES INABILITY. *Obj*. God's sincerity in inviting, beseeching, saying he delights in mercy--"I would have purged them but they would not be purged" [^005-note210], God's declaring he uses means with sinners because he has compassion on 'em, and that he is not willing any should perish but that all should come to repentance. Whitby, pp. 1-6.[^005-note211] ORIGINAL SIN. IMPUTATION. *Obj*. The only crime of the posterity is purely their having been of Adam, and that is wholly from God and not of themselves. Again, nothing makes the connection between Adam's first sin and our guilt more than any other sins of Adam but God's arbitrary imputation of it to 'em, and nothing but God's arbitrary will why he should impute his sin, and not his repentance. Whitby, pp. 7-8.[^005-note212] Concerning the Arminian notion of ELECTION that, when the apostles speak of election, they mean only that by which the professing Christians in those days were distinguished from others, as the nation of Israel of old was elected. This is unreasonable according to their own principles, for if they were elected, and that was the reason they so far embraced the gospel as to become Christians rather than others, then no thanks to them.[^005-note213] Neither were others that continued openly to reject the gospel to blame, and it was in vain to use any means to persuade any to join with the Christian church. Nor were any to blame for not doing it, or to be praised for doing it, etc. And if by election they mean not that by which visible Christians were distinguished from others, but that by which those that had the offers of the gospel and Word, the preaching of the Word etc.: this is most evidently an absurd interpretation of the apostles' words, and inconsistent with them. Show this... And then besides, by their principles it makes in vain all endeavors to spread the gospel: for the gospel will certainly be spread to all nations that are elected, and all such shall have the offers of the gospel whether they took any care of the matter or no. Concerning[^005-note214] the distinction between the SECRET AND REVEALED WILL of God: to show what is the thing intended by it, and then to show the reasonableness and necessity of such a distinction as is intended. There must be a distinction between things that in themselves or in their own nature are agreeable to the nature and inclination of God or the holy disposition of his heart, and what he wills, all things considered. It must be allowed, and they can't deny, that 'tis not contrary to what is truly God's will and desire, all things considered, that yet are contrary to the holy heart of God. If not, then 'tis contrary to what is really his ultimate will, all things considered, that there should be any such thing as sin in the world, and every particular act of sin that comes to pass is against his will. Yea, if there be no distinction to be admitted here, then it follows that every act of sin is surely against his will. If there be no distinction between God's hatred of sin and his will, all things considered, with respect to sin, then the existence or coming to pass of every individual act of sin is as much against that which [is] the ultimate will of God, or his will, all things considered, as he has hatred to it. And we know that his hatred is infinite, and therefore if there be no distinction here, then his ultimate will is infinitely crossed in every act of sin that happens. But an infinite cross to one's will in this sense is infinitely disagreeable: for so much as any one's will is crossed, so much disagreeableness and uneasiness is there; for to have things disagreeable and things contrary to one's will are the same thing, and to bear that which is infinitely disagreeable is the same thing as to bear infinite misery. And if there be no distinction between God's hatred of sin, or his will as to to existing of sin, then God is infinitely crossed every day in millions of millions of instances; which would be to make him infinitely the most miserable of all beings. Dr. Whitby on the Five Points, 2nd ed., p. 39, says when the word ELECTION "is applied to the Jewish converts, it plainly signifies all that had been converted to the Christian faith, as in I Pet. 2:9."[^005-note215] So that it is not persons' having the means of grace that is the thing that is the fruit of sovereign election, but their so far embracing them as to become professing Christians. Again, pp. 41-42:[^005-note216] When Christ saith, many are called but few chosen, these parables plainly relate to the Jews, as Theophylact well notes; and the import of these words is this, that although many of them were called by Christ and his apostles to faith in Him, both in Judea and in the dispersions, yet few of them did or would accept of him as their Savior, or embrace the faith of Christ, as has been fully proved (note on Matthew 28:16, 22:14). So that here the elect, and the believers of the Christian faith, are the same persons. When he saith, Matthew 24:22, that for the elect's sake "those days should be shortened," he styles the faithful left in the midst of the unbelieving Jews "the elect," saith Chrysostom.... When he adds, v. 24, that the false christs and false prophets should do *as much as in them lay* by signs and wonders to deceive the elect, there also we are to understand the persevering Christians. [^005-note217] And when he saith, v. 31, that then God should send his angels to gather his elect "from the four winds, from one end of heaven to the other," the meaning may be either that he would send his angels, as Eusebius and Epiphanius say he did, to warn the Christians in all quarters of Judea to gather themselves together, departing from the unbelieving Jews that they might be preserved from their ruin, or that he would send his messengers with the sound of the gospel to gather together as many as did or would believe from among the nations of the earth. And again, p. 43:[^005-note218] "The election according to grace, mentiond, Romans 11:5, means the whole body of the Jewish converts, even all that did embrace the Christian faith, and were not hardened in or blinded by their prejudices and infidelity; as is apparent from the following words, v. 7, ''The election hath obtained, but the rest were blinded.'" Dr. Whitby, to make out his scheme, makes the word ELECTION signify two entirely different things: one to signify the person's election to a common faith of Christianity, another a conditional election to salvation. But show the unreasonableness of such shifting and varying, and turning into all shapes to avoid the force of Scripture. ELECTION. 'Tis evident the Apostle in Romans 9 han't only respect to God's sovereignty in the election and preferition of nations, because he illustrates his meaning by the instance of a particular person, viz. Pharoah. The exercise of the sovereignty that he speaks of appears by the express words of the Apostle about vessels of mercy and vessels of wrath, vessels of honor and vessels of dishonor; but the vessels of mercy he speaks of [as] mercy prepared to glory [^005-note219]. Them, it is plain, are those that shall be saved, and the vessels of wrath those that perish. See parallel expressions elsewhere. He speaks of those that shall be saved, v. 27. Dr. Whitby says that the words "*prognosis*, foreknowledge; *prothesis*, the purpose; *proorismos*, the foreappointment of God [^005-note220] *none of them relate to particular Persons* (save only when they are used of our blessed Lord and his sufferings for us), but only to churches and nations in general" (p. 48).[^005-note221] Dr Whitby supposes that the Father is said to have ;scGIVEN;rt disciples to Christ in this sense: that the Father gave him the means by which he gained them, i.e. sent him to procure salvation and offer salvation to them and show the terms of salvation, and gave him the miracles by which they were convinced; and that thus it is said, Matthew 16:17, that flesh and blood did not REVEAL to Peter that Jesus was the Christ but his Father which is in heaven (pp. 54-55).[^005-note222] SECRET[^005-note223] AND REVEALED will. Men will sin as sin, and so commit sin, or are the authors and actors of sin as sin; they love it as sin, or secret ends and purposes. God don't will sin as sin, though it be his pleasure that it should come to pass for the sake of the great good that by his disposal will be the consequence. But [^005-note224][^005-note225];ls [^005-note226] qualities[^005-note227] in a constant succession, so that the following acts should constantly and invariably follow the former according to a certain order and law, etc. Now the question is, whether there ben't also a suitableness in such a constitution, that Adam's posterity should be looked upon as so far the same with regard to his covenant and the breach of it as though we are properly looked on as in him in that transaction? UNIVERSAL REDEMPTION. Dr. Whitby says, "Sanctification is only to be ascribed to the blood of Christ by way of motive and engagement" (p. 109, 2nd ed.).[^005-note228] TEXT. John 17:9, "I pray not for the world," i.e. "I ask these blessings that I am now praying for, for believers only, they being blessings proper only to believers." Not but that Christ desired that the world might become believers. Whitby's words are these: "Our Lord says not this absolutely, but only in respect to that very prayer he was then offering up for his apostles, v. 12, in which he was asking those things which could agree to them alone, or to those who were given him out of the world, viz. that they might be consecrated to their apostolical function by that Spirit which the world cannot receive, that their joy from his presence with them might be full; nor is there anything in the whole prayer which is not proper to believers only, i.e. to persons called out of the world" (p. 127, 2nd ed.).[^005-note229] UNIVERSAL REDEMPTION. How unreasonable would it be to say that Christ intentionally died that they might be saved that were then in hell when he died. But the ends and intention of his death was no more limited under the Old Testament than under the New. How unreasonable would it be to say that Christ died intentionally that Judas might be saved, that he had just before openly declared should never have any benefit of his death. And if [^005-note230] had not openly declared this, but had as certainly known it as if he had openly declared it (as he did concerning all that were to perish), it was equally absurd as if he had declared: for if he certainly knew it [of] him, it altered not the case as to his intention, whether that knowledge of his was told to others or kept to himself. What is there that God does DECREE, according to the scheme of these men, and so as to make [them] in any measure consistent with themselves? He don't decree any of the great events of the world of mankind (which are the principal events, and those to which all others are subordinated), because these depend on men's free will. He does not absolutely decree any events wherein the welfare of men is concerned, for if he does, then these things, according to the scheme of these men, can't be the subject of prayer: for according to them, it is absurd to seek or pray for things that we don't know but that God has absolutely decreed and fixed before. We don't know but that he has determined absolutely and unfrusterably from eternity that they shall not be. And then, by their scheme, we can't pray in faith for them. And if God don't decree and order those events beforehand, then what becomes of the providence of God? and what room is there for prayer? Prayer is shut out this way also. (See Whitby, pp. 177, etc.)[^005-note231] We can't reasonably pray for them that are fixed before our prayers, for then our prayers alter nothing: and what signifies it for us to pray? Whitby insists upon it, that we can't pray in faith for the salvation of others if we don't know that Christ died intentionally for their salvation. And if there be no decrees about any of these things, what becomes of the providence of God? These[^005-note232] things we may lay down for certain plain truth and no metaphysical niceties: 1. That when any intelligent being is really crossed and disappointed, and things are counter to what he truly[^005-note233] desires they should be, it is disagreeable to him and diminishes his happiness, etc. The doctrine of PERSEVERANCE was in no measure so clearly revealed under the Old Testament, and therefore it was much more proper for God to insist on the evils of apostacy if it[^005-note234] should be, as in Ezekiel. God treated men then according to the revelation he gave them and the dispensation they were under, as he more frequently exhibited the terms of the covenant of works, saying, "He that doth them shall live in them"; but not because any could obtain life in this way, nor does his so saying suppose it possible. No more does this threatening denounced to the apostacy of the righteous any more suppose the thing threatened possible, than that promise supposes the thing to which the promise is made to be possible. God's threatening something on such a condition does no more imply the condition possible than his promising something on such a condition suppose the condition possible. The Apostle threatened those that were about to flee out of the ships, that if they fled out they should not be saved; and yet it had been revealed to him that they should all be saved. Dr. Whitby supposes it to be absurd to PRAY for that which is already so fixed that it must necessarily come to pass, or for Christ to INTERCEDE for it. His words are as follows: It cannot be proved, that either Christ intercedes or the saints pray more for perseverance to the end, than for their preservation from those sins to which experience and Scripture shows they are obnoxious. So is it as absurd to pray or intercede for that which God hath absolutely decreed from all eternity shall come to pass, as to pray and intercede that the world may not be drowned again, or that Christ may come to judgment, or be the judge of the quick and the dead, or that the bodies of the saints may arise, or for any other thing which shall infallibly come to pass by virtue of God's absolute decree from all eternity; it being, upon this supposition, as certain that this absolute decree concerning their perseverance shall come to pass, though Christ never did intercede, or the saints pray it might do so, or that the other decrees now mentioned shall certainly have their effect with his or our intercession that it may be so (pp. 388-89).[^005-note235] See Book One of Minutes on Moral Agency, p. 68.[^005-note236] DR. WHITBY'S INCONSISTENCE. Again, when it suits the Doctor's turn when writing about perseverance, then all that the apostles write to are true saints, as particularly those the apostle Peter writes to that had *previous faith* (p. 399); and of those that the apostle [^005-note237] wrote the Epistle to the Galatians to (pp. 401-02).[^005-note238] ELECTION. That the Apostle speaks of churches as though they were all elect, I answer, he speaks from a "judgment of charity," as Dr. Whitby himself observes (p. 460).[^005-note239] DR. WHITBY'S INCONSISTENCE. First, he supposes that the Apostle asserts that true saints, when they fall away, 'tis impossible to renew them again to repentance (pp. 394-95); but yet a few pages afterwards, supposes the same Apostle travailed in birth with some that had been true saints and had fallen away that might "renew in them that faith from which they had fallen" (p. 402).[^005-note240] Borrow DR. BARROW'S works.[^005-note241] Also buy or borrow of Dr. Johnson or Mr. Sergeant, Dr. CLARK'S posthumous sermons, which Dr. Johnson speaks of as explaining all texts relating to God's decree of PREDESTINATION.[^005-note242] Concerning[^005-note243] God's SECRET AND REVEALED WILL, see "Miscellanies" no. 170.[^005-note244] God DECREED and ordered that sin of Joseph's brethren, their selling him into Egypt. ORIGINAL SIN. Taylor allows a great extent of the law, so that only irregular desires are sin (p. 213, 2nd ed.)[^005-note245]. He also owns that the law condemns for the breaches of it, without giving any hope of recovery (pp. 211-12).[^005-note246] And he says, p. 207, "The law requireth the most extensive obedience, discovering sin in all its branches," and "that it gives sin a deadly force, subjecting every transgression to the penalty of death." He says, "The law extends to every branch and principle of sin" (p. 213). In the same page, he says sin receives "a destructive force" by the commandmemt. ORIGINAL SIN. Taylor has these words: "The case of those who are under a law threatening death to every sin must be quite deplorable, if they have no relief from the mercy of the Lawgiver."[^005-note247] Consequently, 'tis just with God so to order it that the case of all mankind shall be quite deplorable. For if God may so order it that it will be deplorable unless it be for mercy, then it may be justly made deplorable, and less so, for the [^005-note248][^005-note249] [^005-note250] for[^005-note251] Mr. Taylor, for then men's being corrupted habitually by their own personal transgressions must not be laid to the course of nature neither, but to God: for in this cause, as well as the other, the course of nature is nothing. ORIGINAL SIN. TAYLOR. Romans 5.: to show how the words "judgment," "condemnation," etc. though so often used by this Apostle, yet are never used in Taylor's sense anywhere else, nor anywhere in the New Testament. Original sin. See sermon on John 3:7, about the middle.[^005-note252] Perseverance. If it were left to the freedom of men's own will whether men should persevere in the sense that the Arminians suppose--i.e. to a will not determined by God but self-determined--then it would [be] absurd to pray to God that we may persevere, that he would keep us from falling, and that he would uphold our goings in his paths, etc. Perseverance. If the contrary doctrine be true, then there is no ground of assurance of saints in this world. But show that this is certainly false. Difference between COMMON AND SPECIAL GRACE. To show how there can be no virtue in LOVE or in any other affection, or exercise of mind, or outward action, that is merely from SELF-LOVE. If merely to love one's self ben't virtue, then [to] do something merely from love to one's self cannot be virtuous. If there be no virtue in merely loving ourselves, then to love another wholly and entirely from that principle cannot be virtuous: for in such [a] case, loving another is only a certain kind of exercise of love to ourselves. To love God from no other or higher principle than self love, can't be better than that principle itself from whence it wholly arises: for the exercise can't have anything more or better in it than is in the principles exercised. If love to God and love to our neighbor be wholly and entirely from self-love, then self-love is the sum of these things, as much as the water of the [^005-note253][^005-note254] [^005-note255]. There they should judge the world, judge angels (here see Taylor's *Key*, p. 58).[^005-note256] 'Tis declared that he [that] believes shall not come into condemnation [^005-note257]; that he [that] is born of G. cannot die [^005-note258]; that they that go out were not of us. By faith we enter into rest; [in]Hebrews 3-4, and innumerable other places, this is spoken of as the proper term of final salvation. To observe how unreasonable it is to suppose that the Apostle speaks to the heathen in 2 Corinthians 5:20-21 when he says, "We pray you in Christ's stead, be ye reconciled to God," as Taylor supposes, pp. 94-95, margin, of his *Key*.[^005-note259] ORIGINAL SIN.[^005-note260] 'Tis plain fact that [it] is an universal tendency that mankind are born under to sin and damnation.[^005-note261] And a tendency to eternal misery and destruction is a just exposedness to it, which are the same thing in the present question. Universal fact shows the tendency. The Scripture is plain for the fact that there is no man but what sins. I presume none will deny it. Taylor owns that even good thoughts and workings of heart are sin. And they don't deny that actual sin deserves eternal destruction and is threatened with it.

[^005-note46]: Philip Doddridge (1702-51) was an English Non-conformist and pastor of Northampton whose works were very popular in the colonies. JE here refers to *The Family Expositor, or a Paraphrase and Version of the New Testament, with Notes* (6 vols. London, 1739-56; rep. Amherst, Mass., 1836). His comment on Mark 10:20 appears on p. 237 of the 1836 ed., sec. 137, n. *e*: "It is plain [that the young man] did not understand the spiritual meaning and extent of the *law*, according to our Lord's explication of it in his sermon on the mount; or he would not have pleaded his exact obedience to it. But the Jews in general seem to have thought that, if they abstained from gross crimes, sacrifices might atone for smaller neglects and offences. And this compound seems to have been that righteous of their own, in which, to their final ruin, they trusted for justification before God, in the neglect of the righteousness which is of God by faith."

[^005-note47]: Anne Dutton (d. 1765), *A Letter to the Rev. Mr. J. Wesley. In Vindication of the Doctrines of Absolute...Election, Particular Redemption, Special Vocation, and Final Perseverance* (London, 1742).

[^005-note67]: Whitby, *A Discourse*(London, 1710; 2nd ed., 1735). In pp. 1-6, Whitby points out that the decrees of election and reprobation are mutually supportive, so that "whatsoever Argument holds god against an absolute Decree of *Reprobation*, must certainly destroy the opposite Decree of Absolute Election." JE's objection seems to relate most to Whitby's statement on p. 4 that, according to the decree, reprobates are unable to seek salvation. He writes: if "Reprobates have no sufficient Means to turn their wicked and perverted wills from Sin to God, they can have no sufficient Means either to obtain Salvation, or escape Damnation." In pp. 5-6, Whitby goes on to address two propositions: "1. Absolute Election contains *an eternal, absolute, infallible Decree, that Peter shall believe, repent, persevere unto the end, and be saved*.... 2. Absolute Reprobation is an *absolute, infallible decree, That...*Judas *shall unavoidably fail of obtaining Life eternal*; that this Event shall be so certain, that *he shall never fail to run himself wilfully upon his Damnation*."

[^005-note69]: JE is paraphrasing Whitby from the pages cited. P. 8: "*Adam* being as much in Nature our common Head and Root, and we being as much in his Loyns when he Repented to Salvation,...there is no other Reason, besides God's Arbitrary Will, can be assigned why God should impute his Sin to us to Condemnation, and not impute unto us his Repentance to Salvation."

[^005-note170]: The sermon on John 3:7, dated Jan. 1739/40, has the doctrine, "'Tis no wonder that Christ said that we must be born again." Part three under the doctrine is: "To give the reasons why it is no wonder that Christ said we must be born again." He instructs his listeners to consider what man is by nature, and the result of corruption, namely, that the depravity of man's heart can only be altered by a new birth, and that it is impossible for man to mend his own nature.

Ch. 6, "The particular Honours and Priviledges of Christians, or of those in any Nation who profess Faith in the Son of God, and the Terms signifying those Honours explained," sec. xvii, p. 58: God "has also conferred on *Christians* the Honour of KINGS, as he has redeemed us from the Servitude of Sin, made us Lords of ourselves, and raised us ABOVE others, to sit on THRONES, and to JUDGE, and REIGN over them." Taylor goes on to cite 1 Peter 2:4, 9; Revelation 1:5-6, 10, 2:26-27; 2 Timothy 2:12, and 1 Corinthians 6:2-3.

London, 1735), *1*, pp. 272-95. JE refers especially to p. 275, where Watts states: "Let it be well considered, that this *Sinai-Covenant*...was a distinct thing from the *Covenant of Grace* or that *Gospel*, which secretly ran through all the Dispensations, and which was included in this Dispensation also... The great Apostle, in his Epistle to the *Romans*, and *Galatians*, and *Hebrews*, is often teaching them, that this *Sinai-Covenant*, this *Law of Moses*, with all the Ceremonies of it, could not give them Life,Galatians 3:21. that is, Pardon of Sin, and eternal Salvation.";en

[^005-note203]: Philip Doddridge (1702-51) was an English Non-conformist and pastor of Northampton whose works were very popular in the colonies. JE here refers to *The Family Expositor, or a Paraphrase and Version of the New Testament, with Notes* (6 vols. London, 1739-56; rep. Amherst, Mass., 1836). His comment on Mark 10:20 appears on p. 237 of the 1836 ed., sec. 137, n. *e*: "It is plain [that the young man] did not understand the spiritual meaning and extent of the *law*, according to our Lord's explication of it in his sermon on the mount; or he would not have pleaded his exact obedience to it. But the Jews in general seem to have thought that, if they abstained from gross crimes, sacrifices might atone for smaller neglects and offences. And this compound seems to have been that righteous of their own, in which, to their final ruin, they trusted for justification before God, in the neglect of the righteousness which is of God by faith."

[^005-note231]: Beinecke copy, pp. 179-82, in which Whitby criticizes the doctrine of limited atonement, which in his view "unworthily reflects upon our Good and Gracious God" and depicts God as "having no Bowels of Compassion, no Drop of Mercy." Furthermore, limited atonement implies that "God by sending his Son to be the *Saviour* of the World,...had no other primary End than the glorifying himself in the Salvation of Men" (pp. 177-78). The death of Christ *could* have procured salvation for all, *if* God had so planned, but according to the decrees, the reprobate are excluded from any benefits of Christ's death. This, for Whitby, renders God to be "full of Guile, Deceit, and Insincerity" (p. 179).;en

## "Notes on Coming of Christ" Notes on Coming of X

Jonathan Edwards

Notes on the Coming of Christ The MS (BRBL, f. 1231) can be tentatively dated to the mid-1740s because of cross-references to other manuscripts, such as sermons and a "Miscellanies" entry. It is written on a salvaged letter cover addressed to Edwards at Northampton, each side divided into three columns. Concerning the Captivity of the Children of Isr the Jews into Babilon & there being there Continued till the old Generation was wasted & Gods pouring out his spirit on the young Generation. Ezekiel. 20. 34. ---- 44. THE CAPTIVITY INTO BABILON. was for the Humbling the young Generation & fitting them for mercy as was the Travel in the wilderness. as appears by Lamentations 3:27. Notes on the Coming of Christ Concerning the captivity of the children of the Jews into Babylon and their being there continued 'till the old generation was wasted, and God's pouring out his spirit on the young generation,Ezekiel 20:34:44. THE CAPTIVITY INTO BABYLON was for the humbling the young generation and fitting them for mercy, as was the travail[^006-note7] in the wilderness, as appears by Lamentations 3:27. The introduction of the GLORIOUS TIMES. Then shall be fulfilled that in Psalms 149:6-9, of which that in Deuteronomy 7:1-2 was a type (not that this will be done in a way inconsistent with meekness and love). That which we have an account of, Revelation 19, is a fulfillment of it. An then shall be fulfilled that in Revelation 2:26-27; then shall that be fulfilled, Isaiah 41:15-16. Near the CONCLUSION, speaking of the renovation and perfecting the elect world: The mosaic creation was but the beginning of the work of creation, the first dawnings of the light, the embryo of [what] God intended should be when he had brought things to their perfection. 'Tis only to prepare men and angels to see the second and more glorious work, seeing the first creation and the great glory of it, and then seeing the glory that excels. When writing of the IMPETRATION[^006-note8] of redemption by Christ's death, then see sermon on Zechariah 13:1.[^006-note9] CHRIST'S ASCENSION the new occasion of glory to the highest heavens: then see note on John 14:2, those words, "I go to prepare a place for you."[^006-note10] God's granting an anticipation of future glory to the church, as to the church under the old testament, of gospel blessing to the church in the apostles' days, of the blessings of the glorious times, etc.: see notes on Romans 8:23, "first fruits of the Spirit."[^006-note11] Christ as the head of the congregation will lead the way in the songs of heaven to the Father, as Moses did in the song of the congregation of Israel. Hence we read of the song of Moses and the Lamb [^006-note12], as David led the way in the songs of Israel, and was the sweet Psalmist of Israel. The Captain shall lead the way in their triumph. As he was the mouth of the disciples in prayer in his humiliation, so shall he be in praise in his exaltation. Where the Psalmist in the Psalms speaks of his praising God in the great congregation,[^006-note13] it is often to be understood of Christ. Christ, as the head of the church, is the head of them; [they] are a congregation of worshippers.[^006-note14] THE GRAND APOSTACY after the millennium will be most like the fall of the angels in heaven that ever was, and its punishment will be most like theirs. Its beginning will be spiritual pride, the world having so long been righteous, so universally and so generally been sanctified in infancy, that men will not be so sensible of the corruption of nature and such a desperate proneness to wickedness. ONE TRIAL OF THE ANGELS' obedience before Christ's exaltation was that, 'till then, they were in a great measure kept in the dark as to God's grand design and scheme in those great works, wherein they were his ministers from age to age. Ephesians 3:9-10. See "Miscellanies," no. 994.[^006-note15] See sermons on Hebrews 2:7-8 and Rev. 14:4.[^006-note16] The devil when he sees his kingdom falling, as his lust of reigning and his pride will be exceedingly contradicted, at first he will use great subtilty in opposing, and also rage. But when he finds all his subtilty from time to time frustrated and his kingdom apace falling, as though all were going down and he could do nothing to help it, it may be expected that he will be in such a rage and terror withal, as will perfectly befool him. His rage will be exceeding great, so as to make him even drunk with it, and he will act like one mad. And so swiftly will that great army of spiritual wickedness in high places come headlong and in great confusion down from heaven to hell. GLORIOUS TIMES. Then shall be fulfilled the promise made to Abraham that he should be the heir of the world, and that in him and his seed all the families of the earth should be blessed [^006-note17]. Then shall be fulfilled that promise, that the meek shall inherit the earth, Psalms 37 and Matthew 5. END of the World. God sees fit that the whole world should first have the light of the gospel, Matthew 24:14.

## "Notes on Conversion from Various Authors" Notes on Conversion

Various

"NOTES ON CONVERSION FROM VARIOUS AUTHORS"

Edwards evidently began this folio notebook (BRBL, Box 21, f. 1262) shortly--very shortly, to judge by watermark, ink, and handwriting--after the death of his grandfather in February 1729, since Stoddard is referred to in the past tense several times. The notebook seems to have been conceived as a primary source book to contain quotes from reputable authors on the subject of conversion, perhaps for use in an intended essay on the subject. A close examination of the themes reveals that the notebook is related to Edwards' ongoing scrutiny of his grandfather's views of conversion, as seen especially in "Miscellanies" entries on humiliation, conversion, and related topics dating from 1729 and 1730. Here, for instance, Edwards collects quotes that militate against Stoddard's idea that one should be able to locate the time of one's conversion and that humiliation is not gracious.

The works and authors referred to in this document are all standards of the Reformed and Puritan traditions. Two works quoted here are mentioned early in Edwards' "Catalogue," namely, Dutch Calvinist Peter van Mastrict's *Theoretico-Practica Theologia* (1699), which Edwards came to regard as one of his favorites, and English Dissenter Joseph Alleine's *Alarm to Unconverted* *Sinners* (1672). William Ames' famous *Medulla Theologica* was a text that Edwards studied in college. Edwards also quotes from Giles Firmin's *Real Christian* (1670), an important statement on conversion. A more obscure work that passed under several titles was *The Life of Mr. Henry Gearing, or, The Character and Trial of a Real Christian*, published by John Shower. Edwards probably obtained this book from his father, Timothy, since it is listed in his library inventory.[^007-note1] The names of Puritan worthies such as John Preston, Thomas Shepard, and John Nalton are cited, but the references to at least the latter two of these authors were attained secondhand through Gearing's *Life*.

*****

Edwards quickly abandoned this notebook and did not make subsequent entries. It is composed of a folio single-folded signature, which has a London Arms/Crowned GR(L) watermark. This watermark first appears in a sermon on Deuteronomy 7:10, dating from April or May 1729, and then in a batch of sermons from July to December, a fact which supports a starting point for this notebook very soon after Stoddard's death. Edwards divided each page down the middle by a vertical line. He numbered only columns 1-6 on pp. 1-3, with column 7 on the left-hand side of p. 4 being a continuation of column 2 on p. 1. Some of the columns have several entries, while others have only a title. The right-hand column on p. 4 is completely empty.

[^007-note17] Complains of, & Condemns himself Romans 7. & all his Inventory is Poor & miserable & wretched & blind and naked Revelation 3:17. He sees a world of Iniquity in his Holy things and Calls his once Idolized Righteousness but flesh, & Loss, & dogs meat & would not for a thousand worlds be found in himself [remainder of col. blank] [blank][col. 2] "NOTES ON CONVERSION FROM VARIOUS AUTHORS" (edited) 1. CONCERNING KNOWING THE TIME OF CONVERSION. Dr. Preston, an author that Mr. Stoddard greatly admired[^007-note18] for his skill in matters of experience, advises that this be no stick with persons about their conversion, that they don't know the time.[^007-note19] Mr. Firmin speaks of it as a strange doctrine, that men must know the time, and says to this purpose: Did ever any divine, worthy of the name of a divine, teach such doctrine?[^007-note20] Mr. Williams of Hatfield[^007-note21] says that Mr. Stoddard liked this author. Mr. Alleine, in his *Alarm to the Unconverted*, p. 84, seems to suppose that a man may be converted and not know the time. His words are these: "When was the time, where was the place, or what was the means, by which this thorough change of the new birth was wrought in his soul? Speak, Conscience. Or if thou canst not tell time and place, canst thou show Scripture evidence, that the work is done?"[^007-note22] 2. WHETHER HUMILIATION BE NOT GRACE AND BEN'T ANTECEDENT TO CONVERSION, AND OPINIONS ABOUT THE NATURE OF HUMILIATION. Dr. Ames accounts, 'tis repentance that usually goes before a sensible act of faith (*Medulla*, ch. 26, §35).[^007-note23] Mr. Nalton, a famous London minister that flourished about the middle of the last century, speaks of humiliation as sorrow for sin. His words are: "After convincing, the Spirit of God humbles the sinner; he is humbled to the dust. This sorrow and humiliation is in some more, some less." See in Mr. Gearing, his *Trial* of himself, p. 45.[^007-note24] Van Mastrict supposes there to be three things preparatory to actual faith and repentance, viz. 1. Contrition, which the Apostle calls "the spirit of bondage to fear" [^007-note25], which he supposes is meant when the Psalmist speaks of a contrite heart, and Christ, when he speaks of those that labor and are heavy laden; which consists in the sinner's sense of the filthiness of his sins, and the anger of God and fear of punishment, and sense of his own folly and madness in sinning as he has done, and great grief and sorrow accompanying of it, and confession of sins before God and man and indigation against himself. 2. Humiliation, by which the contrite person, in the thoughts of his sins and misery that he is in danger of, is vile and abject in his own eyes, and condemns himself and acknowledges he deserves eternal damnation, is filled with shame and acknowledges himself to be wholly unworthy of any mercy. 3. Saving desperation, whereby a person is sensible that he has nothing to pay for his own deliverance, becomes poor in spirit, and that we can do nothing for our own deliverance. And he supposes in those that are to be converted, those preparatory exercises flow from a principle of grace already infused in regeneration. Though there may be after a sort materially the same things in reprobates, and that there are the very same things after actual faith and repentance, and to a much greater degree.[^007-note26] Mr. Alleine of Taunton, in his *Alarm to the Unconverted*, speaks of a man's being brought off from his own righteousness, as what is wrought in conversion and by converting grace. Speaking of the terms from which we are turned in conversion, he enumerates them thus, p. 32: "The terms from which we turn in this motion of conversion, are sin, Satan, and the world, and our own righteousness." Which fourth he speaks to particularly, in p. 38 of the last, in these words: Fourthly, your own righteousness. Before conversion, man seeks to cover himself with his own fig leaves (Philippians 3:6-7), and to lick himself whole with his own duties (Micah 6:6-7). He is apt to trust in himself (Luke 16:15 and Luke 18:9), and set up his own righteousness, and reckon his counters for gold, and not submit to the righteousness of God (Romans 10:3). But conversion changes his mind; now he casts away his filthy rags, and counts his own righteousness, but a menstruous cloth: he casts it off, as a man would the verminous tatters of a nasty beggar (Isaiah 64:7). Now he is brought to poverty of spirit (Matthew 5:3), complains of and condemns himself (Romans 7), and all his inventory is poor, and miserable, and wretched, and blind, and naked (Revelation 3:17). He sees a world of iniquity in his holy things, and calls his once-idolized righteousness, but flesh, and loss, and dog's meat, and would not for a thousand worlds be found in himself.[^007-note27] 3. WHETHER A DISTINCT ACT OF FAITH IN CHRIST BE ALWAYS THE FIRST SENSIBLE AND REMARKABLE ACT OF GRACE. Dr. Ames says that "repentance is wont to be perceived first before faith," in his *Medulla*, ch. 26, §35.[^007-note28] 4. WHETHER THERE BE NO DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THE GODLY AND SOME OTHER MEN WITH RESPECT TO A CONSCIENTIOUS BEHAVIOR. Mr. Alleine, who was accounted eminent for his skill in the nature of conversion, gives this as a sign of true grace: he says, Where there is true grace, there is an hearty willingness to part with every sin. Wherever there is this breach made between sin and the soul, it is grace that hath made it. [^007-note29] Reason saith, "I ought to turn"; Conscience saith, "I must turn," and yet nothing [may] follow: but when the heart saith, "I will turn to God," then the work is done. Reason saith, "These idols ought not to stand"; Conscience saith, "These lusts must be subdued": but when the will saith to them, "Get you hence," there is a work of grace begun. This willingness discovers itself to be prevailing---1. When a man is truly willing to part with sin, there will be resolutions against it; he takes part with God against sin, and uses all means for the conquering of it. 2. This resolution will bring forth resistance. See in the Life of Mr. Gearing, in the *Character and Trial of a Real Christian*, pp. 18-19.[^007-note30] Mr. Shepard himself mentions it as one of the three things wherein a child of God goes beyond an hypocrite, viz. "no hypocrite, or unregenerate man, though he goes never so far, let him do never so much, but he lives in one sin or other, secret or open, little or great." As Mr. Gearing gives an account, in his *Trial* of himself, p. 22.[^007-note31] 5. WHETHER STRONG TERRORS ARE NECESSARY TO GO BEFORE CONVERSION. 6. OF THE MANNER OF CONVERSION, AND CIRCUMSTANCES OF THE CONVERTED. In the account that is given of the conversion of Mr. Gearing's niece in Mr. Gearing's *Life*, under the title of *The Character and Trial of a Real Christian*, she gives an account that, under hearing a sermon upon that text, Matthew 11:28, "Come unto me," etc. she was much affected in the hearing of it. She felt sin to be a burden to her conscience, and would fain have rest to her soul, was sensible of her want of Jesus Christ, of her absolute necessity to be interested in him. She began to see his worth and excellency, as the chief of ten thousands, altogether lovely; and then had hungrings and thirstings after him, so that she could say, "None but Christ, give me Christ, or else I die." And afterward, in hearing a sermon upon that text, Matthew 13:45, "The kingdom of heaven is like a merchant man seeking goodly pearls," etc. she received much light and comfort, and saw more of the transcendent excellency of Jesus Christ, who was the pearl of great price, and thought she could most willingly part with all, even life itself, for him. And that after this she was tempted to question the truth of the holy Scriptures, and after that was assaulted with the most horrid atheistical and blasphemous temptations; and that she was under great terrors and apprehensions of the wrath of God and fears of hell. And after all this, first began the practice of the duty of secret prayer by the direction of one that she advised with. But after this, she had the most remarkable comforts, and the gracious and joyful influences of the Spirit which she experienced were very great and remarkable.[^007-note32]

[^007-note1]: Apart from JE's personal problems with Stoddard's view of conversion, it is interesting to note that at the same time that he began this document, his father and sisters were collaborating on a spiritual biography, modelled in part after Gearing's *Life*, of sister Jerusha Edwards, who died of diptheria in Dec. 1729, and that Timothy was keeping a private notebook in which he copied passages that assured him of Jerusha's sainthood.;en

[^007-note2]: Note that JE refers to Stoddard in the past tense throughout the document, which, along with the rounded and neat hand, in all likelihood places it after Stoddard's death, perhaps in the very early 1730s. The precise source of this reference is uncertain, but in *The Saints Qualification* (London, 1633), the noted Puritan John Preston (1587-1628), master of Emmanuel College, Cambridge, and preacher at Lincoln's Inne, discusses "the New-Creature" and throughout emphasizes a retrospective apprehension of a general change of nature. In answering the question, "How shall I know when my Nature is altered?" (p. 109), Preston writes, "Consider what constancy, what evennesse, what equality is in your Nature; for if there be another Nature given you, if you be other men, you doe not act another person, for then you may be ready to put it off, and lay it aside, but your Nature is altered, and so your carriage will be constant."

[^007-note3]: Giles Firmin (1617-97) emigrated to New England, but returned to England to become the pastor of Stratford, Essex, only to be ejected in 1662 because of non-conformity. The statement JE refers to occurs in *The Real Christian, or a Treatise of Effectual Calling* (London, 1670). In ch. 1, "Concerning Preparation of the Soul for Christ, in General," under the 3d position, "The wayes of God in converting, or drawing the soul to Christ, are very secret, and in preparatory work very various," Firmin notes that one way in which God's work is various is "as to *time*." Some can "tell the time, the day," or "the signal Providence of God" by which they they were converted, but others "cannot tell the *time*, when began the work; and this have been to some a long time an objection against the truth of their regeneration, because they cannot tell the *time* of their new birth;...as if there were any ground in Scripture for such a Position, *All that are new born know the time of their new birth*: What Divine, that did deserve the name of a Gospel-Minister, did ever deliver such a Doctrine?"

[^007-note4]: William Williams (1665-1741), pastor of Hatfield, Massachusetts, and JE's uncle.

[^007-note5]: **marginal.** [-]

[^007-note6]: Joseph Alleine (1634-1668), English Non-conformist and minister of Taunton. He is most remembered for his *Alarme to Unconverted Sinners* (London, 1672; rep. 1678). The quote appears on p. 102 of the 1678 ed.

[^007-note7]: William Ames (1576-1633), Puritan, fled to Holland, where he attended the Synod of Dort and taught theology at the University of Franeker. The *Medulla Theologica* was originally published in Latin in 1623, but translated into English in 1642. Ch. 26 is "Of Calling," but the section to which JE refers is numbered 34 in the English translation: "Repentance is wont to be perceived before Faith: because a sinner cannot easily perswade himselfe that he is reconciled to God in Christ, before he feele himselfe to have forsaken those sins which did separate him from God" (p. 114).

[^007-note8]: John Shower, *Some Account of the Holy Life and Death of Mr. Henry Gearing...with the Trial and Character of a Real Christian, Collected Out of His Papers, for the Examination of Himself* (rep. Boston, 1720), p. 49. The reference is to John Nalton, a Puritan preacher.

[^007-note9]: JE paraphrases from Peter Van Mastrict (1630-1706), *Theologia theoretica*-*practica* (1699).

[^007-note10]: Alleine, *Alarme*, 1678 ed., pp. 40, 46.

[^007-note19]: The precise source of this reference is uncertain, but in *The Saints Qualification* (London, 1633), noted Puritan John Preston (1587-1628), master of Emmanuel College, Cambridge, and preacher at Lincoln's Inne, discusses "the New-Creature" and throughout emphasizes a retrospective apprehension of a general change of nature. In answering the question, "How shall I know when my Nature is altered?" (p. 109), Preston writes, "Consider what constancy, what evennesse, what equality is in your Nature; for if there be another Nature given you, if you be other men, you doe not act another person, for then you may be ready to put it off, and lay it aside, but your Nature is altered, and so your carriage will be constant."

## Note to Fragment of a Notebook on the Signs of Grace Notes on Signs (HSP)

Jonathan Edwards

NOTE TO FRAGMENT OF A NOTEBOOK ON THE SIGNS OF GRACE The following document is a fragment of a notebook, devoted mainly to the signs of grace and related topics, consisting of five octavo leaves. Together, the fugitive leaves form pages 9-17 of the notebook. The first leaf of the notebook to which these leaves once belonged is apparently the one at Jonathan Edwards College, Yale University.[^008-note1] Beyond the content, there are few clues on which to make assumptions about the disassembled notebook. Originally it was of a considerable length, since at one point in the extant leaves there is a reference to "p. 42." Also, the entries are not deleted with vertical lines, Edwards' usual manner of indicating he had used a given passage or that it was no longer of interest to him. The handwriting of the leaves dates from the 1740s. We can speculate that this may have been one of a series of notebooks, of which "No. 7" is extant, that Edwards drew up for writing *Religious Affections*.

*****

Fugitive leaves (pp. 9-17) from a notebook on Religious Affections. N.d. [c. 1745-46]. HSP, Dreer Autograph Coll., 126:1, American Clergy, v. III (3 leaves); Etting Collection, 26, p. 36 (1 leaf). Faith also is the first Exercise of Grace by which they must emerge out of a dead frame by They must Look to X for help from their darkness & deadness & blindness as the poor blind man. so it is their duty to believe & trust in X to be delivered from darkness.

Proverbs 26. 23. burning Lips & a wicked Heart are like a Potsherd covered with silver dross. difficulty of DISCERNING who are converted Practice PRAC TICE the safest Rule sign.] Common Illuminations & Satans delus and Instances of the spirit of FRAGMENT OF A NOTEBOOK ON THE SIGNS OF GRACE LIVING ON FRAMES. Doubting of a thing may arise from unbelief when it comes from a sight of abundance of corruption in the heart. For this may be when persons are far from being in a carnal frame.[^008-note25] Faith also is the first exercise of grace by which they must emerge out of a dead frame. They must look to Christ for help from their darkness and deadness and blindness, as the poor blind man. So it is their duty to believe and trust in Christ to be delivered from darkness. Another evidence that discoveries or a ;scSENSE OF GODS LOVE;rt is not the primary foundation of holy dispositions and affections, or not that wherein grace does fundamentally consist, is that a person may be without this and yet be perfectly holy, yea, in the highest and most exalted and supreme exercises of holiness, as Christ on the cross. LOVING FOR EXCELLENCY. If there be any such thing as their being beautiful and lovely in themselves otherwise than as tending to an interest, then it will follow that they may and ought to be loved from that excellency. For if there be such a beauty, then it may be seen as it is; and if it be seen as it is, it will appear beautiful and amiable in itself, as it is, and consequently will be delighted in and loved: for that is implied in its appearing beautiful. INFINITE OBLIGATION. Observe the difference between being infinitely obliged to a thing and being obliged to an infinite thing. PRACTICAL EXERTIONS. The highest sign is grace, as carried forth into its practical exertions or into the fruit that is produced in practice. BEAUTY OF HOLINESS. See 2 Chronicles 20:21. SPIRITUAL TASTE.[^008-note26] Objection concerning the approbation there is in natural men of virtue, etc. In answer, to show that the natural approbation of men's consciences of virtue, and condemnation of vice, is a thing entirely distinct from the relish that is in a good man of virtue. As appears by this: that at the same time that this testimony of conscience will be in its greatest perfection, viz. at the day of judgment, wicked men will be furthest from that relish; and hatred of holiness and a wicked taste and inclination will be in its greatest perfection. NATURAL MEN'S LOVE TO VIRTUE. The love of natural men is as their abhorrence is. A natural man abhors the sight of a rattlesnake; it is an unpleasant sight to him: which is only because he habitually connects it with the idea of hurtfulness to him. So he hates vice no other way, and loves many kind of virtues in the same, when all is originally to be resolved into self-love. There is this, and also the testimony of his conscience concerning the punishableness of vice and rewardableness of virtue. The testimony of a man's conscience of the punishableness of a thing is a different thing from a sense of the odiousness of it; and the testimony of the conscience of the rewardableness of [virtue] is different from a sense of the beauty {of virtue}. This is manifest in what will be at the day of judgment. And besides, there is a kind of association or habitual connection by education. PRACTICE. In Psalms 105, last verse, after rehearsing over God's wondrous works that he had wrought for Israel, this is, in the fine, mentioned as God's aim in all: "*That they might observe his statutes, and keep his laws*." See p. 42.[^008-note27] PRACTICE. Making Christ the minister of sin and improving free redemption as an encouragement to sin. Jeremiah 7:9-10, "Will ye steal, murder," etc. "and come and stand before me in this house, which is called by my name, and say, We are *delivered* to do all these abominations?" The end of the JUDGMENT of the Last Day will be [to] make persons manifest, not only to the world but to their own CONSCIENCES. Jude 1:14-15, "Behold, the Lord cometh with ten thousands of his saints, to execute judgments upon all, and to convince all that are ungodly among them of all their ungodly deeds which they have ungodly committed, and of all their hard speeches which ungodly sinners have spoken against him." EVANGELICAL HUMILIATION. Isaiah 46:12, "Hearken to me, ye stouthearted, that are far from righteousness." Psalms 119:21, "Thou hast rebuked the proud that are cursed, which do err from thy commandments." True saints are of a MERCIFUL spirit. The word commonly used in the Old Testament for saints is *Kasidim*. See Psalms 116:15 and other places in Buxtorf.[^008-note28] PRACTICE. He that gives his heart to Christ must needs give his body to him. It would be as absurd to suppose the heart and outward practice, soul and body, to be separated in the true and faithful spouse of Christ, so that she should indeed give her heart to him but serve other lovers with her body, as the heart and body in a temporal spouse, so that she should be indeed true and faithful in her heart and yet give her body to other lovers. Would not all the world call that man a fool, that should regard the pretenses of his wife that pretended that she was constant and true in her conjugal affections to him, and yet lead the practice of a whore? PRACTICE. Proverbs 14:2, "He that walketh in his uprightness feareth the Lord: but he that is perverse in his ways despiseth him." BOLDNESS for CHRIST. When the angels of God came to Jacob, and he called the name of the place "Mahanaim," i.e. "two armies," the host of angels was one army, and Jacob's train going against Esau and his host of persecuters of the church was the other army, representing the church militant; and as manner of warning against enemies and persecuters, they encounter Esau and his 400 men, not with swords and bloody weapons but with liberal presents, with great kindness, with respect, with love and friendliness and humble respect, and with entreaties, and with prayers and wrestlings with God. PRACTICE. HUMILIATION. Proverbs 26:16, "The sluggard is wiser in his own conceit than seven men that can render a reason." How this is true in matters of religion. How there is a sort of religion and pretended high experience and great affections that don't issue in a diligent and laborious constant practice of religion, but in settled slothfulness and neglect of the difficult parts of Christian practice that yet exceedingly lifts men up with a high conceit of their own goodness and high experience, and causes 'em to look on themselves vastly better than others, and to look down with contempt on them that are real, humble, modest Christians. HUMILIATION. SPIRITUAL PRIDE. Proverbs 26:12, "Seest thou a man wise in his own conceit? there is more hope of a fool than of him." TALKING MUCH AND ZEALOUSLY NO SIGN. Proverbs 26:23, "Burning lips and a wicked heart are like a potsherd covered with silver dross." Difficulty of DISCERNING who are converted, PRACTICE the safest sign. Common illuminations and instances of the spirit of [^008-note29]

## "Of the Decrees of God" Of the Decrees of God

Jonathan Edwards

"Of the Decrees of God"

This outline, covering both sides of a piece of scrap paper, probably dates from the (late?) 1740s. Since the treatise projected here was intended to deal subtantially with efficacious grace, we can speculate that the ""Efficacious Grace" notebooks and the section on the subject in the "Controversies" notebook were draft materials for this proposed work. Note particularity the similarity of themes in the notebooks and the outline, for example, God does not decree evil; the need of a governing providence; the principle of grace is decisive, immediate and supernatural; and the difference of ""orthodox" from Arminian.

*****

The manuscript (BRBL, f. 1240) is a quarto leaf folded over once and written on pp. 1-3. Edwards, Jr. made some minor changes on the manuscript.

[^009-note1]

TITLE of the Decrees of God and his decisive disposab Provi especially with Respect to mens salvation I Book Part of the decrees of G. in General Chap I Explaining the nature of 1.[^009-note2] what I mean by a decree divine dec 2. The difference between Gods decrees of Good & Evil. N.B. G. never properly decrees Evil as He decrees it is good. Chap 3. God * here several Things may be Observed It dont [^009-note3] belong to the definition of a divine decree that it should be eternal ---- who that all divi whether divine decrees are Eternal is a matter to be proved It comes into the nature of divine decrees they are for some End It & that the Event is some decided by themselves[^009-note4] where there is no decisive Providence there is Governing Governmt or no no disposing ordering or governing Providence what he dont determine beforehand he dont order at all it comes to pass without his ordering or will & so without his Governmt.[^009-note5] Chap. II. That Gods decrees are universal that He decrees all Events whatsoever Of the Universality of Gods decrees § 1. That G. decrees shewing in [^009-note6] general that God decrees all Events prove it from the foreknowl. & other rational arguments & from SS. 2. In particular that d He decrees the Acts of the wills of Intelligent Creatures Chap. III. That Gods decrees are all eternal PART II. concerning God's decisive Grace in the Conversion of s of parti of a sinner. He[^009-note7] first what is included in Efficacious . 1 The main thing meant by Efficacious the word Efficacious is This its being decisive This seems to be the main Question 2. Its being immediate and arbitrary in that sense as not to be [^009-note8] limited to Laws of nature -- 3. That the Principles[^009-note9] are no supernatural in that sense that they are entirely different from all that is in the Heart before Conversion. 4. That they are infused and not contracted by Custom & Exercise. 5. That the Change is Instantaneous & not Gradual. These four last Heads may be subdivisions of of a 2d General Head so that the division may be thus 1. The main Thing meant by that it its being[^009-note10] decisive. 2. The another thing wherein they differ is the manner wherein the orthodox suppose the work to be the in the[^009-note11] immediate & supernatural. the four last of the Heads mention'd above [^009-note12] may be subdivisions of the last. [^009-note13]

TITLE: Of the Decrees of God and His Particular Decisive Disposals, Especially with Respect to Men's Salvation. Ist Part. Of the Decrees of God in General. Chap I. Explaining the nature of: 1.[^009-note14] What I mean by a divine decree. Here several things may be observed: It don't belong to the definition of a divine decree, that it should be eternal; whether divine decrees are eternal is a matter to be proved. It comes into the nature of divine decrees that they are for some end, and that the event is decided by themselves.[^009-note15] Where there is no decisive providence there is no government, or no disposing, ordering, or governing providence. What He don't determine beforehand, he don't order at all; it comes to pass without his ordering or will, and so without his government.[^009-note16] 2. The difference between God's decrees of good and evil. N.B. God never properly decrees evil; as He decrees, it is good. Chap. II. Of the Universality of God's Decrees. 1. Showing in general that God decrees all events. Prove it from the foreknowledge and other rational arguments, and from Scripture. 2. In particular, that He decrees the acts of the wills of intelligent creatures. Chap. III. That God's Decrees Are All Eternal. PART II. Concerning God's Decisive Influence and Grace in the Conversion of a Sinner. Show[^009-note17] first what is included in efficacious: 1. The main thing meant by the word efficacious is this: its being decisive. This seems to be the main question. 2. Its being immediate and arbitrary in that sense, as not to be limited to laws of nature. 3. That the principles[^009-note18] are supernatural in that sense, that they are entirely different from all that is in the heart before conversion. 4. That they are infused and not contracted by custom and exercise. 5. That the change is instantaneous and not gradual. These four last heads may be subdivisions of a second general head. So that the division may be thus: 1. The main thing meant by that its being[^009-note19] decisive. 2. Another thing wherein they differ is the manner wherein the orthodox suppose the work to be immediate and supernatural.[^009-note20] The four last of the heads mentioned above may be subdivisions of the last.

## “Concerning Efficacious Grace” (Remarks, 1796)

**The following excerpts appear in** **Remarks on Important Theological Controversies** **(Edinburgh, 1796) as part of a compilation of excerpts from Edwards’ corpus, assembled most likely by Jonathan Edwards, Jr. or John Erskine, under the title “Concerning Efficacious Grace.” Of the 100 entries--drawn from the “Controversies” notebook, the three notebooks on “Efficacious Grace,” and various “Miscellanies”--the texts of twelve entries, and part of a thirteenth, namely, from the second paragraph of §39 through §51, are not to be found anywhere in Edwards’s printed or manuscript corpus. If Edwards’ earlier editors did not corrupt these excerpts to the point where they are thoroughly unrecognizable, we must conclude that these thirteen entries are unique to this document.**

**It is impossible to ascertain whether these entries came from one missing notebook, but, given that the other entries are assembled in groups by sources, it seems likely. For example, the second paragraph of §39 bears some textual similarity to an entry in the “Controversies” notebook (WJE 21:300-1), where Edwards refers to “Paper on Free Will, no. 8,” which is no longer extant. (According to Thomas Schafer’s research notes from 1957, “secs. 39-51 are from Fan Bk. No. VIII, which I have not yet found among the MSS.”)** [^010-note1]

**Given the lack of provenance, it is difficult to assign a general date. However, §47 refers to** **Freedom of the Will** **, a first draft of which he completed in April 1753; and in §48 Edwards states, “See what I have written in my book of** **Original Sin** **,” which makes this a very late composition indeed, since we know that he completed that treatise in May 1757.**

* * * *

The entries on efficacious grace are part of Ch. IV in the **Remarks**and run from p. 219 to p. 226. Comparison of other entries with their

<!-- p.   -->

manuscript originals shows sometimes significant differences, and we can assume that these unique entries were subjected to the same sort of “improvement.” Later editions, including the Worcester reprint and the Worcester revised edition (ed. Hickman), which most modern reprints reproduce, besides perpetuating textual corruptions, skip §44 as it appears in the original, as well as others in the series outside the range of §§39-51.
