### “Concerning Efficacious Grace”

§ 39 **. . . .** [^010-note3] So that there are two things relating to the doctrine of efficacious grace, wherein lies the main difference between the Calvinists and Arminians as to **this doctrine.** **First** **, That the grace of God is de-** termining and decisive as to the conversion of a sinner, or a man’s becoming a good man, and having those virtuous qualifications that entitle to an interest **in Christ and his salvation.** **Secondly** **, That the pow-** er and grace and operation of the Holy Spirit, in, or towards, the conversion of a sinner, is immediate: that the habit of true virtue or holiness is immediately implanted or infused: that the operation goes so far, that a man has habitual holiness given him instantly, wholly by the operation of the Spirit of God, and not gradually by assistance concurring with our endeavours, so as gradually to advance virtue into a **prevailing habit. And besides these,** **Thirdly** **, It is held** by many of late, that there is no immediate interposition of God; but that all is done by general laws. The former is that which is of greatest importance or consequence in the controversy with Arminians, (though the others are also very important); and this, only, is what I shall consider in this place; perhaps the others may be considered, God willing, in some other discourse. § 40. **Concerning what the Arminians say, that** these are speculative points: all devotion greatly depends on a sense and acknowledgment of our depen-

<!-- p.   -->

dence on God. But this is one of the very chief things belonging to our dependence on God: how much stress do the Scriptures lay on our dependence on God! All assistance of the Spirit of God whatsoever, that is by any present influence or effect of the Spirit; Spirit; any thing at all that a person that is convert- [221] ed from sin to God, is the subject of, through any immediate influence of the Spirit of God upon him or any thing done by the Spirit, since the completing and confirming the Canon of the Scriptures, must be done by a physical operation, either on the soul or body. The Holy Spirit of God does something to promote virtue in men’s hearts, and to make them good, beyond what the angels can do. But the angels can present motives; can excite ideas of the words of promises and threatenings, &c. and can persuade in this way by moral means; as is evident, because the devils in this way promote vice. § 41. **There is no objection made to God’s produ-** cing any effects, or causing any events, by any immediate interposition, producing effects arbitrarily, or by the immediate efforts of his will, but what lies equally against his ordering it so, that any effects should be produced by the immediate interposition of men’s will, to produce effects otherwise than the established laws of nature would have produced without men’s arbitrary interposition. I beg the reader’s attention to the following quota-

<!-- p.   -->

tions.--” That otherwise, the world cannot be the “ object of inquiry and science, and far less of imi- “ tation by arts: since imitation necessarily presup- “ poses a certain, determinate object, or fixed ascer- “ tainable relations and connexions of things; and “ that, upon the contrary supposition, the world must “ be absolutely unintelligible. Nature, in order to “ be understood by us, must always speak the same “ language to us. It must therefore stedfastly ob- “ serve the same general laws in its operations, or “ work uniformly, and according to stated, invari- “ able methods and rules. Those terms, order, beau- “ ty, general good, &c. plainly include, in their mean- “ ing, analogy; and constancy, uniformity amidst va- “ riety; or, in other words, the regular observance “ of “ of general settled laws, in the make and œconomy, [222] “ production, and operations or effects, of any object “ to which they are ascribed. Wherever order, fix- “ ed connexions, or general laws and unity of de- “ sign take place, there is certainty in the nature of “ such objects, and so knowledge may be acquired. “ But where these do not obtain, there can be no- “ thing but unconnected, independent parts. All “ must be disorder and confusion; and consequently, “ such a loose, disjointed heap of things, must be an “ inexplicable chaos. In one word, science, pru- “ dence, government, imitation, and art, necessarily “ suppose the prevalence of general laws through- “ out all the objects in nature to which they reach. “ No being can know itself, project or pursue any

<!-- p.   -->

“ scheme, or lay down any maxims for its conduct, “ but so far as its own constitution is certain, and “ the connexion of things relative to it are fixed and “ constant. For so far only are things ascertainable; “ and therefore, so far only can rules be drawn from **“ them.”** **Turnbull’s Mor. Phil** **. Part. I.** **Introd** **.** [^010-note4] “ The exercise of all moral powers, dispositions “ and affections of mind, as necessarily presuppose an “ established order of nature, or general laws settled “ by the author of nature with respect to them, as “ the exercise of our bodily senses about qualities and “ effects of corporeal beings do with regard to them. “ We could neither acquire knowledge of any kind, “ contract habits, or attain to any moral perfection “ whatsoever, unless the author of our nature had “ appointed and fixed certain laws relating to our “ moral powers, and their exercises and acquisitions.” **Ibid** **. p. 13, 14.** [^010-note5] **Yet this Turnbull strenuously holds** a self-determining power in the will of man. Such like arguments, if they are valid against any interposition at all, will prevail against all interposition of God or man, and against the interposition of God ever to bring the world to an end, or amend it; and prove that all shall be according to general laws. And they might as well argue, that the making of the world too was by general laws. If it be said, that that it is of great importance and absolute necessity, [223] that God should at last interpose and rectify the course of nature:--I answer, this is yielding the

<!-- p.   -->

point, that, in cases of great importance, it is reasonable to suppose there may be an interposition that may be arbitrary, and not by general laws. § 42. **It is not necessary that men should be able,** by the connexions of things, to know all future events; nor was this ever in the Creator’s designs. If it had been so, he could have enabled them to know the future volitions of men, and those events that depend upon them, which are by far the most important. § 43. **The nature of virtue being a positive thing, can** proceed from nothing but God’s immediate influence, and must take its rise from creation or infusion by God. For it must be either from that, or from our own choice and production, either at once, or gradually, by diligent culture. But it cannot begin, or take its rise from the latter, viz. our choice, or voluntary diligence. For if there exist nothing at all of the nature of virtue before, it cannot come from cultivation; for by the supposition there is nothing of the nature of virtue to cultivate, it cannot be by repeated and multiplied acts of virtuous choice, till it becomes an habit. For there can be no one virtuous choice, unless God immediately gives it. The first virtuous choice, or a disposition to it, must be immediately given, or it must proceed from a preceding choice. If the first virtuous act of will or choice be from a preceding act of will or choice, that preceding act of choice must be a virtuous act of choice, which is contrary to the supposition. For then there would be a pre-

<!-- p.   -->

ceding act of choice before the first virtuous act of choice. And if it be said the first virtuous act of choice is from a preceding act of will, which is not virtuous, this is absurd. For an act of will not virtuous, cannot produce another act of will of a nature entirely entirely above itself, having something positive in it [224] which the cause has nothing of, and more excellent than it is; any more than motion can produce thought or understanding; or the collision of two bodies can produce thought; or stones and lead can produce a spirit; or nothing can produce something. § 44. **A notable text for the proof of determining** grace, are those words in Jerem. xxxi. 18, 19. “ Turn thou me, and I shall be turned; for thou art the Lord my God. Surely after that I was turned, I repented; and after I was instructed, I smote **upon my thigh.”** [^010-note6] § 45. **As to man’s inability to convert himself--In** them that are totally corrupt, there can be no tendency towards their making their hearts better, till they begin to repent of the badness of their hearts. For if they do not repent, they still approve of it; and that tends to maintain their badness, and confirm it. But they cannot begin sincerely to repent of the badness of their hearts, till their hearts begin to be better; for repentance consists in a change of the mind and heart. So that it is not men’s repentance that first gives rise to their having a better heart; and

<!-- p.   -->

therefore it cannot be any tendency in them to make their hearts better, that gives rise to it. The heart can have no tendency to make itself better, till it begins to have a better tendency; for therein consists its badness, viz. its having no good tendency or inclination. And to begin to have a good tendency, or, which is the same thing, a tendency and inclination to be better, is the same thing as to begin already to be better. And therefore the heart’s inclination to be good, cannot be the thing that first gives rise to its being made good. For its inclination to be better, is the same thing with its becoming better. § 46. **If there be any immediate influence or ac-** tion of the Spirit of God at all on any created beings, in in any part of the universe, since the days of the a- [225] postles, it is physical. If it be in exciting ideas of motives, or in any respect assisting or promoting any effect, still it is physical; and every whit as much so, as if we suppose the temper and nature of the heart is immediately changed. And it is as near akin to a miracle. If the latter be miraculous, so is the former. § 47. **Who ever supposed, that the term** **irresistible** **** was properly used with respect to that power by which an infant is brought into being; meaning, irresistible by the infant? Or who ever speaks of a man’s **waking out of a sound sleep** **irresistibly** **, meaning,** that he cannot resist awaking? Or who says, that

<!-- p.   -->

Adam was formed out of the dust of the earth irresistibly? See what I have said of the use of such **terms as** **irresistible, unfrustrable** **, &c. in my Inquiry** **about Liberty.** [^010-note7] § 48. **The opponents of efficacious grace and phy-** sical operation, may be challenged to show that it is possible that any creature should become righteous without a physical operation, either a being created with the habit of righteousness, or its being immediately infused. See what I have written in my book **of** **original sin** **, in those sections wherein I vindicate** the doctrine of original righteousness, and argue, that if Adam was not created righteous, no way can be **invented, how he could ever become righteous.** [^010-note8] § 49. **As to that, Matthew vii. 7. “ Seek and ye** shall find;” it is explained by such places as that, Deut. iv. 29. “ But if from thence thou shalt seek the Lord thy God, thou shalt find him, if thou seek him with all thy heart and with all thy soul.” And by Deut. xxx. 2--6. “ If thou shalt return unto the Lord thy God, and shalt obey his voice, with all thy heart and with all thy soul;--the Lord thy God will circumcise thine heart, and the heart of thy seed, to love love the Lord thy God with all thy heart, and with [226] all thy soul:” which is very parallel with that, ” to him that hath shall be given.” § 50. **The Scripture teacheth, that holiness, both**

<!-- p.   -->

in principle and fruit, is from God. “ It is God who worketh in you, both to will and to do of his **good pleasure.”** [^010-note9] Phil. 2:13. Edited version

 And Prov. xvi. 1. “ The prepara-tion of the heart in man, and the answer of the tongue, is from the Lord.” Comparing this with other parts of the book of Proverbs, evinces that it is a moral preparation, and the answer of the tongue in moral regards, that is meant. § 51. **Reason shows, that the first existence of a** principle of virtue cannot be from man himself, nor in any created being whatsoever; but must be immediately given from God; or that otherwise it never can be obtained, whatever this principle be, whether love to God, or love to men. It must either be from God, or be an habit contracted by repeated acts. But it is most absurd to suppose, that the first existence of the principle of holy action, should be preceded by a course of holy actions. Because there can be no holy action without a principle, or holy inclination. There can be no act done from love, that shall be the cause of first introducing the very existence of love.

### “Concerning Efficacious Grace” (edited version)

§39 **. . . .** [^010-note10]

**So that there are two things relating to the doctrine of efficacious grace, wherein lies the main difference between the Calvinists and Arminians as to this doctrine.** **First** **, that the grace of God is determining and decisive as to the conversion of a sinner, or a man’s becoming a good man, and having those virtuous qualifications that entitle to an interest in Christ and his salvation.** **Secondly** **, that the power and grace and operation of the Holy Spirit, in, or towards, the conversion of a sinner, is immediate: that the habit of true virtue or holiness is immediately implanted or infused; that the operation goes so far, that a man has habitual holiness given him instantly, wholly by the operation of the Spirit of God, and not gradually by assistance concurring with our endeavours, so as gradually to advance virtue into a prevailing habit. And besides these,** **Thirdly** **, it is held by many of late, that there is no immediate interposition of God; but that all is done by general laws.**

The former is that which is of greatest importance or consequence in the controversy with Arminians (though the others are also very important); and this, only, is what I shall consider in this place; perhaps the others may be considered, God willing, in some other discourse.

§40. **Concerning what the Arminians say, that these are speculative points: all devotion greatly depends on a sense and acknowledgment of our dependence on God. But this is one of the very chief things belonging to our dependence on God: how much stress do the Scriptures lay on our dependence on God! All assistance of the Spirit of God whatsoever, that is by any present influence or effect of the Spirit; anything at all that a person that is converted from sin to God, is the subject of, through any immediate influence of the Spirit of God upon**

<!-- p.   -->

**him or anything done by the Spirit, since the completing and confirming the canon of the Scriptures, must be done by a physical operation, either on the soul or body.**

The Holy Spirit of God does something to promote virtue in men’s hearts, and to make them good, beyond what the angels can do. But the angels can present motives; can excite ideas of the words of promises and threatenings, etc., and can persuade in this way by moral means; as is evident, because the devils in this way promote vice.

§41. **There is no objection made to God’s producing any effects, or causing any events, by any immediate interposition, producing effects arbitrarily, or by the immediate efforts of his will, but what lies equally against his ordering it so, that any effects should be produced by the immediate interposition of men’s will, to produce effects otherwise than the established laws of nature would have produced without men’s arbitrary interposition.**

I beg the reader’s attention to the following quotations, that otherwise, the world

cannot be the object of inquiry and science, and far less of imitation by arts: since imitation necessarily presupposes [. . .] a certain, determinate object, or fixed ascertainable relations and connexions of things; and that, upon the contrary supposition, the world must be absolutely unintelligible. Nature, in order to be understood by us, must always speak the same language to us. It must therefore steadfastly observe the same general laws in its operations, or work uniformly, and according to stated, invariable methods and rules. Those terms, order, beauty, general good, etc., [. . .] plainly include, in their meaning, analogy and constancy, uniformity amidst variety; or, in other words, the regular observance of general settled laws, in the make and economy, production, and operations or effects, of any object to which they are ascribed. Wherever order, fixed connections, or general laws and

<!-- p.   -->

unity of design take place, there is certainty in the nature of such objects, and so knowledge may be acquired. But where these do not obtain, there can be nothing but unconnected, independent parts. All must be disorder and confusion; and consequently, such a loose, disjointed heap of things, must be an inexplicable chaos. In one word, science, prudence, government, imitation, and art, necessarily suppose the prevalence of general laws throughout all the objects in nature to which they reach. No being can know itself, project or pursue any scheme, or lay down any maxims for its conduct, but so far as its own constitution is certain, and the connection of things relative to it are fixed and constant. For so far only are things ascertainable; and therefore, so far only can rules be drawn from them. (Turnbull’s**Moral Philosophy,** Pt. I, **Intro**.)[^010-note11]

**[T]he exercise of all moral powers, dispositions and affections of mind, as necessarily presuppose an established order of nature, or general laws settled by the author of nature with respect to them, as the exercise of our bodily senses about qualities and effects of corporeal beings do with regard to them. [. . .] We could neither acquire knowledge of any kind, contract habits, or attain to any moral perfection whatsoever, unless the author of our nature had appointed and fixed certain laws relating to our moral powers, and their exercises and acquisitions. (** **Ibid** **. pp. 13-14.)** [^010-note12] ****

Yet this Turnbull strenuously holds a self-determining power in the will of man. Such-like arguments, if they are valid against any interposition at all, will prevail against all interposition of God or man, and against the interposition of God ever to bring the world to an end, or amend it; and prove that all shall be according to general laws. And they might as well argue, that the making of the world too was by general laws. If it be said, that it is of great importance and absolute necessity, that God should at last interpose and rectify the course of nature: I answer, this is yielding the point, that, in cases of great importance, it is reasonable to suppose there may be an

<!-- p.   -->

interposition that may be arbitrary, and not by general laws.

§42. **It is not necessary that men should be able, by the connections of things, to know all future events; nor was this ever in the Creator’s designs. If it had been so, he could have enabled them to know the future volitions of men, and those events that depend upon them, which are by far the most important.**

§43. **The nature of virtue being a positive thing, can proceed from nothing but God’s immediate influence, and must take its rise from creation or infusion by God. For it must be either from that, or from our own choice and production, either at once, or gradually, by diligent culture. But it cannot begin, or take its rise from the latter, viz. our choice, or voluntary diligence. For if there exist nothing at all of the nature of virtue before, it cannot come from cultivation; for by the supposition there is nothing of the nature of virtue to cultivate, it cannot be by repeated and multiplied acts of virtuous choice, till it becomes an habit. For there can be no one virtuous choice, unless God immediately gives it. The first virtuous choice, or a disposition to it, must be immediately given, or it must proceed from a preceding choice. If the first virtuous act of will or choice be from a preceding act of will or choice, that preceding act of choice must be a virtuous act of choice, which is contrary to the supposition. For then there would be a preceding act of choice before the first virtuous act of choice. And if it be said the first virtuous act of choice is from a preceding act of will, which is not virtuous, this is absurd. For an act of will not virtuous, cannot produce another act of will of a nature entirely above itself, having something positive in it which the cause has nothing of, and more excellent than it is; any more than motion can produce thought or understanding; or the collision of two bodies can produce thought; or stones and lead can produce a spirit; or nothing can produce something.**

§44. **A notable text for the proof of determining grace, are those words in Jer. 31:18-19, “Turn thou me, and I shall be turned; for thou art the Lord my God. Surely after that I was turned, I repented; and after I was instructed, I smote upon my thigh.”** [^010-note13]

§45. **As to man’s inability to convert himself: In them that are totally corrupt, there can be no tendency towards their making their hearts better, till they begin to repent of the badness of their hearts. For if they do not repent, they still approve of it; and that tends to maintain their badness, and confirm it. But they cannot begin sincerely to repent of the badness of their hearts, till their hearts begin to be better; for repentance consists in a change of the mind and heart. So that it is not men’s repentance that first gives rise to their having a better heart; and therefore it cannot be any tendency in them to make their hearts better, that gives rise to it. The heart can have no tendency to make itself better, till it begins to have a better tendency; for therein consists its badness, viz. its having no good tendency or inclination. And to begin to have a good tendency, or, which is the same thing, a tendency and inclination to be better, is the same thing as to begin already to be better. And therefore the heart’s inclination to be good, cannot be the thing that first gives rise to its being made good. For its inclination to be better, is the same thing with its becoming better.**

§46. **If there be any immediate influence or action of the Spirit of God at all on any created beings, in any part of the universe, since the days of the apostles, it is physical. If it be in exciting ideas of motives, or in any respect assisting or promoting any effect, still it is physical; and every whit as much so, as if we suppose the temper and nature of the heart is immediately changed. And it is as near akin to a miracle. If the latter be miraculous, so is the former.**

§47. **Whoever supposed, that the term** **irresistible** **was properly used with respect to that power by which an infant is brought into being; meaning, irresistible by the infant? Or who ever speaks of a man’s waking out of a sound sleep** **irresistibly** **, meaning, that he cannot resist awaking? Or who says, that Adam was formed out of the dust of the earth irresistibly? See what I have said of the use of such terms as** **irresistible, unfrustrable** **, etc., in my Inquiry about Liberty.** [^010-note14]

§48. **The opponents of efficacious grace and physical operation, may be challenged to show that it is possible that any creature should become righteous without a physical operation, either a being created with the habit of righteousness, or its being immediately infused. See what I have written in my book of** **Original Sin** **, in those sections wherein I vindicate the doctrine of original righteousness, and argue, that if Adam was not created righteous, no way can be invented, how he could ever become righteous.** [^010-note15]

§49. **As to that, Matt. 7:7, “Seek and ye shall find”; it is explained by such places as that, Deut. 4:29, “But if from thence thou shalt seek the Lord thy God, thou shalt find him, if thou seek him with all thy heart and with all thy soul.” And by Deut. 30:2-6, “If thou shalt return unto the Lord thy God, and shalt obey his voice, with all thy heart and with all thy soul; the Lord thy God will circumcise thine heart, and the heart of thy seed, to love the Lord thy God with all thy heart, and with all thy soul”: which is very parallel with that, ”to him that hath shall be given.”** [^010-note16]

§50. **The Scripture teacheth, that holiness, both in principle and fruit, is from God. “It is God who worketh in you, both to will and to do of his good pleasure.”** [^010-note17] **And Prov. 16:1, “ The preparation of the heart in man, and the answer of the tongue, is from the Lord.”**

<!-- p.   -->

**Comparing this with other parts of the book of Proverbs, evinces that it is a moral preparation, and the answer of the tongue in moral regards, that is meant.**

§51. **Reason shows, that the first existence of a principle of virtue cannot be from man himself, nor in any created being whatsoever; but must be immediately given from God; or that otherwise it never can be obtained, whatever this principle be, whether love to God, or love to men. It must either be from God, or be an habit contracted by repeated acts. But it is most absurd to suppose, that the first existence of the principle of holy action, should be preceded by a course of holy actions. Because there can be no holy action without a principle, or holy inclination. There can be no act done from love, that shall be the cause of first introducing the very existence of love.**

## Note To “Paper on Justification” (Notes on Imputation and Free Grace)

The title for this manuscript is Edwards’ own, as given in “On Free Will.” Possibly, its contents were notes for revisions to “Justification by Faith Alone” as Edwards prepared the discourse for publication. This conjecture is supported by dates of the documents used for the notes (“Justification by Faith Alone” was preached in Nov. 1734, and the notes are no earlier than Oct. 1734) as well as similar ink and hand. Parallels with the Justification discourse are provided in the annotations. Yet there are also significant portions that do not relate to the discourse. The manuscript is interesting for both these unused portions and for formative passages on justification, imputation, and grace. With “On Free Will” and “Book of Minutes on the Arminian Controversy” (Gazeteer Notebook), this MS forms a phalanx of polemic pieces relating to Edwards’ anti-Arminian campaign. His unexplained references, such as “they,” “their scheme,” even simply “it,” refers to Arminian writers and their position on justification. * * * * * The manuscript is in two pieces. First is a broadside proclamation for a Massachusetts Thanksgiving, by Gov. Jonathan Belcher, dated Oct. 3, 1734 (printed in Boston). Edwards started on the blank side, dividing it into six columns (secs. 1-12), and continued on the printed side, dividing it into two columns that wrap around the printed text (secs. 13-18). The top and right (if the printed side is up) edges are badly tattered, resulting in lacunae. Some passages have a vertical, or “use,” line drawn through them. The second part of the manuscript is formed by two pieces of paper, stitched together.

<!-- p.   -->

One piece is an undated prescription for Sarah Edwards from Samuel Mather, Northampton’s physician. Edwards wrote secs. 19-20 on the blank side. The other is a letter from the Rev. Nehemiah Bull of Westfield, dated Nov. 4, 1734 (Letter B003). On the backside of this paper, folded unevenly, Edwards made two short columns of notes; however, their relation to the previous entries is uncertain.

## “PAPER ON JUSTIFICATION”

**[To]** [^013-note1] **show, first, that it doth [derog]ate from the freedom [of gr]ace, and to show plainly how more grace appears in the other.**

**[Seco]ndly,** [^013-note2] **to show that it derogates [from] the honor of the Redeemer [and] gives that honor to man that [belon]gs to the Redeemer, contrary to the design of the gospel, which is to abase man and to ascribe all glory to Christ.**

To show that we are justified by the imputation of Christ’s righteousness. To show what I mean by imputation. “By the obedience of one shall many be made righteous” [Rom. 5:19]. Whether it be active or passive, yet still it is under the notion of obedience. ‘Tis as his obedience that it makes us righteous; and if so, ‘tis under the notion of a righteousness.

**The** [^013-note3] **distinction of active and passive obedience in this case is utterly improper, for all obedience is active. All obedience, considered under the notion of obedience, is active; and considered under the notion of obedience, ‘tis righteousness. ‘Tis contrary the obedience there [is] in suffering. So far as ‘tis in obedience, ‘tis active; if considered as obedience, ‘tis considered [as]** [^013-note4] **act. Indeed, the sufferings of Christ may be considered under a twofold consideration: either as a part of his obedience [or] a part of his righteousness, [and] so it is meritorious; or as properly his suffering, and so it is a satisfaction or propitiation for sin.**

Show how is it possible that by the righteousness of one, another should be made righteous, without imputation. One can’t be made righteous by the righteousness of another, unless [th]e righteousness be made his or be looked upon as his. If a man is accepted as righteous, that righteousness on

<!-- p.   -->

the account [of] which he is accepted, so becomes [hi]s righteousness by that acceptance; or, which is the same thing, is accepted as his.

Their scheme is self-contradictory, for it makes a man justified where he is: for it supposes a man [to] have a right to salvation before he is justified. For, first, they suppose that a man is not justified till he is become godly, or has special and saving grace; but, secondly, they hold that he has a right to salvation before he has special grace, viz. by a good improvement of common grace. For they say he has a right to special grace, which implies salvation; but he that has a right to that which imples a right to salvation, has already a right to salvation, and can challenge it. He can challenge it already; as, for instance, he that has a right to be looked upon as the king’s eldest son, he therein has a right to the crown because, being the king’s eldest son implies the other.

So that, according to this scheme, a man is justified not by evangelical obedience, but by the good improvement of common grace. The absurdities, viz.: first, that he is justified before he is; second, justified by that which is no godliness, which in itself is an absurdity; and thirdly, that yet he is justified only by his godliness.

They say that God has promised special grace to a good improvement of common grace, and salvation to special grace. Therefore, I say, that he has in effect promised salvation to only a good improvement of common grace, so that he can then immediately challenge it without further delay. Thus, for instance, if one man promises another that if he will do such a thing, and at the same promises, that if he has the possession of another thing, and that if he has that other thing, he will give him another, and when he has that, another: I say, that in such a case, as soon as ever he has performed the first condition, he has a right to all the other things that follow one

<!-- p.   -->

another, and can challenge ‘em then without further delay; because as soon as that first is performed, that other stands bound to bestow all the rest. So that the proper condition of salvation is the good improvement of common grace, which they themselves allow is no godliness.

But they will say he is not justified before the end of his life.

**But they hold that sins are pardoned before the end of life. Vid. Paper on Free Will, no. 8.** [^013-note5]

**If** [^013-note6] **eternal life be given for the sake of what we have done, or from respect to our excellency, then ‘tis not given for the sake of Christ. (Here to show the inconsistency of these two). And if it ben’t given for the sake of Christ, then Christ is but a partial Redeemer or Savior; he is but half a Savior. He don’t redeem us unto God, for he don’t purchase eternal life for us.**

**Here it may be said that [Christ] purchases heaven for us, in that he purchases that our sincere obedience may be accepted as the condition of salvation, and so purchases an opportunity for us.** [^013-note7]

But is that properly purchasing heaven for us, to purchase an opportunity for us to purchase it? But if it be, if Christ’s righteousness or merits ben’t imputed to us, all that he does is to satisfy for our sins and pay our debt for us; there is no positive purchase of any good. His purchasing that our imperfect obedience should be accepted, is only his satisfying for the imperfections of our obedience; or, which is the same thing, satisfying for the sins mixt with our obedience. He only sets us free from hell, and leaves us to purchase heaven for ourselves. So that he don’t properly purchase heaven for us in any respect, as he that only pays a price to deliver another from slavery can’t be said to purchase an estate for him too, merely because thenceforward he has opportunity to work for himself, and

<!-- p.   -->

so by the price of his own hard labor to purchase an estate for himself.

So that according to this scheme, the possession and inheritance of the saints has not been ourchased by Christ, contrary to Scripture.

According to their scheme, our own obedience is properly the price of heaven: for ‘tis that upon the account of our own goodness, or for the sake of it, that heaven is bestowed. Now that which is rendered to God, upon the account of which he gives the possession, is certainly the price of that possession.

So that the saints in heaven have no reason to thank Christ for purchasing heaven for ‘em, or redeeming them to God by his obedience, and purchasing for them to be kings and priests; and therefore we find ‘em in the Revelation praising Christ without a cause. So that all that Christ does for us, is only to deliver us from us; indeed, that is an occasion of our having opportunity to do something for ourselves. But he does no more for us by his redemption.

**Their notion of a new law is absurd. They say that Christ died to satisfy the law for us. I ask, what law? If they say, the old, I answer, how then is free grace manifested in accepting our imperfect obedience, if God can’t justly not accept it? He could not die to satisfy that for us, for that we never were under. (Vid. Reflections Concerning Free Will, [no. 6].)** [^013-note8]

“On Free Will”: “I answer: how could Christ die to satisfy the first law for us, when, according to their scheme, we never broke that law? For according to them, we never broke that law in any sense. We never broke that law under Adam, for they say Adam was not our representative, and the guilt of his breaking of the law don’t belong to us. And we never broke it in our persons, for according to their scheme we never were under it: and how can we break a law that we were never under? And therefore, how could Christ die to satisfy the law for us, who never broke it, nor even were under it?

“It may be they will answer, Christ died to satisfy the law for us that we might not be under the law, and that we might be delivered from it. But what need of Christ’s dying that we might not be under the law, when, according to their scheme, ‘twould in itself be unjust that we should be under that law, because in our present state we are not able to keep it? What need therefore of Christ’s dying, that we might not be under a law that it would be unjust that we should be under, whether Christ died or no?”

 If they say that he died to satisfy the new law, I answer, that according to their scheme [it] is to be answered by us in our sincere obedience. Christ abolished the old law and procured a new constitution, which man is required to fulfill himself. He is to stand for himself, and to fulfill that new law himself by sincere obedience, in the same manner that Adam was to have fulfilled the old law by perfect obedience.If Christ satisfies the new for us, as well as the old, then I would

<!-- p.   -->

inquire why he don’t deliver us from the new law too by satisfying for it, as well as his satisfying the old. If we have broken the new law too, as well as the old, so that Christ must satisfy it for us, why don’t his satisfaction deliver us from that as well as the other, and so make way for a third law? They say Christ’s satisfying the old law abolishes it; and if so, why don’t his satisfying the new law abolish that too?

They say that Christ came into the world to die, [to] make way that our imperfect obedience might be accepted. But what need of that, when it would be unjust in itself that any other obedience than impartial obedience should be accepted? What need of Christ’s dying to make way for God’s accepting such an obedience? As it would be in itself unjust in him not to accept, is there any need of Christ’s dying to persuade God not to do unjustly?

**According to their scheme, God would do unjustly not to accept of our imperfect obedience; and that because, say they, ‘tis the best obedience that we have power to perform, and because it was none of our fault that we have not power to perform better, because Adam’s sin is not imputed to us. Vid. Reflections on Free Will, [nos. 5-7].** [^013-note9]

IMPUTATION of Christ’s righteousness. **They perhaps may answer to my argument for the imputation of Christ’s righteousness, the need of fulfilling the law, that Christ by his obedience fulfilled the law for us, that the law, being fulfilled, might be abolished.**

Answer: **this don’t alter the case whether the law be abolished or not, yet if that obedience was performed in our stead--which is the thing I have been arguing for--then it is accepted in our stead, or, which is the same thing, accepted as if we had performed it; which is what I mean by imputation.**

**If it was performed in** [^013-note10] **our stead, then** [^013-note11] **Christ performed it as our**

<!-- p.   -->

**representative, and if he had not performed it, we should have been obliged to perform it ourselves: for there was no need of Christ’s performing in our stead that that did not belong to us;** [^013-note12] **but now, because he has performed it in our stead, are not obliged, then ‘tis accepted for us, or accepted as if we had performed it.**

**In** [^013-note13] **the beginning of my argument about imputation, to show what I mean by imputation of Christ’s righteousness, and to add that I don’t insist on the word “imputation” if the thing be allowed agreeable to this interpretation.**

FAITH NOT GOSPEL OBEDIENCE, **as is evident by the 4th [chapter] of Romans: for there in that chapter, speaking of Abraham’s being justified by faith, he explains it to be a dependence on the sufficiency and faithfullness of God with reference to the promised seed. What can be plainer? ‘Tis exceeding evident that the Apostle is speaking of the same faith that Abraham before was spoken of as justified for: for there at the end of that explanation, he adds, vv. 22-24, “And therefore it was imputed to him for righteousness.” God in that promised seed had respect chiefly to Christ, by this very Apostle’s own testimony. And we have no reason to doubt but that Abraham knew and understood that Christ was respected in it, saw Christ’s day held forth in that promise, and rejoiced in a dependence on the fulfillment of the promise as it respected Christ, from what Christ says: “Abraham saw my day: and he saw it, and was glad” [John 8:56]. Abraham by that very act of faith received Christ, by the Apostle’s own testimony, Heb. 11:19--speaking there of Abraham’s faith in the very same promise with respect to his seed as is here spoken of, adds, “from whence also he received him in a figure.”**

And it need not seem strange, for by Christ’s own testimony Abraham saw his day and rejoiced in the sight of it [John 8:56].

<!-- p.   -->

IMPUTATION. [^013-note14] **We are expressly told that we are justified by the obedience of Christ as a righteousness, in the 5th [chapter] of Romans. Compare 18th and 19th verses.**

If Christ has suffered the penalty of the law for us or in our stead, then it will follow that his suffering that penalty is imputed to us; i.e. that his suffering the penalty for us is accepted for us and in our stead, so that we have the benefit of it.

**And so, if Christ fulfilled the law by obeying of it for us and in our stead, it will in like manner necessarily follow that his obedience is imputed or accepted for us and in our stead, as though we had performed it. [...]** [^013-note15]

IMPUTATION. [^013-note16] **Another argument is that justification is by the righteousness or obedience of Christ. Our being received into God’s favor and to a right to eternal life, if it be in reward for the obedience of Christ, then ‘tis not the reward of our obedience. If our own obedience be the thing on the account of which we are acepted, then we are not acepted on the account of the obedience of another.**

In what respect soever Christ is our Savior, that doubtless excludes our being our own saviors in the same sense. And therefore, [if] he be our Savior in that sense that he works out a righteousness on the account of which we are accepted and justified, then ‘tis not we ourselves that work out a righteousness on the account of which we are accepted and justified; because if we can be our own saviors in the same sense that Christ is, then Christ’s salvation is in vain, according to the Apostle’s own way of arguing: “If righteousness come by the law, then Christ is dead in vain” [Gal. 2:21]. Christ is rendered of no effect to you. Doubtless it is Christ's prerogative to be our Savior, in that sense wherein he is our Savior.

<!-- p.   -->

**It will follow that Christ’s righteousness is imputed to us, because Christ is our second Adam; and as Adam’s sin is imputed to us, so is Christ’s righteousness imputed to us (I Cor. 15:22). And the Apostle himself testifies that we are justified in Christ as we are condemned in Adam, and by the obedience of one as we are by the disobedience of the other; but we are condemned by the disobedience of Adam as that is imputed to us (Rom. 5:12).** [^013-note17]

FREE GRACE. [^013-note18] **It certainly shows a great goodness and grace, and that by which the creature is more obliged, for God to show mercy to it and to give it a right to eternal blessings, upon an odious, sinful, unworthy creature, without being drawn or influenced by any loveliness or excellency in the creature, than if it were drawn or influenced.**

**For an explanation of the term IMPUTATION, see Mastricht’s** **de Justificatione** **, §4.** [^013-note19]

The word IMPUTE is used in Scripture for putting upon one’s account. Philem. 1:18, “Put that upon my account,” the same word as in Rom. 4. ‘Tis in the original, “impute.” So Rom. 4:8, sin is not imputed, that is, is not reckoned to our account; and Rom. 4:6, the word must be used there in the same sense: righteousness is imputed without works, is the same as reckoning righteousness to a man’s account that has none of his own; for to be without works and without righteousness are the same things. But if he has no righteousness of his own, then imputing righteousness to him must be reckoning some other righteousness to his account that is not his own: for if righteousness is imputed to him, then there is a righteousness that is his. But if it ben’t of his own working, it must be some other.

**The word in the original, as a certain excellent divine observes, is taken from the custom of merchants, whereby anything in their account, either debt or credit, is transferred from one man’s account to another. Vid.**

<!-- p.   -->

**Mastricht,** **de Justificatione** **, §12.** [^013-note20] **[...]** [^013-note21]

[ Obj. [^013-note22] 1. If Christ has the] **reward of his own obedience, how came we to have the reward of it?**

Ans. **Christ wrought out his obedience as a public person. He died for Christ’s mystical, etc. Vid. “Miscellanies.”** [^013-note23]

Obj. 2. **If the saints have the happiness of heaven as the reward of Christ’s righteousness, then it will follow that they all have an equal degree of happiness in heaven.**

Ans. **Vid. “Miscellanies.”**

It will be said perhaps that the salvation ben’t given directly as the REWARD of our obedience, but ‘tis our interest in Christ’s satisfaction and righteousness is given as the reward of our obedience. But how absurd is that! This is to ascribe as much to our obedience, as if we ascribed salvation to our obedience immediately without any righteousness of Christ: for ‘tis as great a thing, ‘tis as great a reward, to have Christ given us, and his righteousness as a reward, as to have salvation immediately given as a reward. ‘Tis evident, because it implies it. If this ben’t too great a reward for our obedience, then salvation is not; and so there would be no need of Christ’s righteousness. That which is given as a reward for something, is evermore given out of respect to the excellency of that thing: for that is the very notion of a reward.

REWARD. **The thing that the Scripture guards against is an imagining that ‘tis our own goodness that recommends us or entitles us to the favor of God. But to suppose that God gives us an interest in Christ as a reward of any of our goodness, such a reward of our goodness is as great an argument that ‘tis our goodness that recommends us to the favor of God, as if God immediately bestowed salvation as the reward of our own goodness. And indeed,**

<!-- p.   -->

**it implies it: for if God gives us an interest in Christ and his benefits as a reward of our obedience, then he gives salvation as the reward of our obedience, for the former implies the latter; and indeed, it implies it, as the greater does the less. So that indeed it exalts our obedience more to suppose that God would give us Christ and his benefits, than if he should give us salvation immediately as the reward of our obedience: for to have Christ’s salvation in him is a greater benefit than to have salvation alone without him.**

If God gives men an interest in Christ as a reward of their obedience, then it follows that God don’t accept of persons into his favor for the sake of Christ’s righteousness: for this argues that God has accepted into his favor, already antecedent to their being in Christ. For according to this scheme, they have an interest in Christ’s righteousness, given as a fruit of God’s favor; and therefore, God’s favor is not the fruit of that. Christ’s righteousness is given as a fruit of God’s favor, because ‘tis given as a reward for what is in them. If God bestows an infinite blessing on a person, surely ‘tis an evidence that he is already in his favor and is already accepted.

’Tis a contradiction to suppose that anything should be rewarded, and not out of respect to the excellency of it. To suppose justification given as a reward of anything in us, is the very thing that the Apostle opposes. To give justification for anything in us as a work, can’t be better explained than to give justification in reward of what we do.

IMPUTATION. [^013-note24] **It was necessary that his obeying the law should be accepted for us, instead of our obeying, for the same reason as that his suffering the penalty of the law should be accepted as though we had suffered the penalty, viz. to answer the law. One is as much required in order to**

<!-- p.   -->

**answer the law as the other. This is given as the reason why Christ “was made a curse for us”: because the law threatened a curse to us. Gal. 3: 10-13, “For it is written, Cursed is every one that hangeth on a tree.” But Christ will deliver us “from the curse of the law, being made a curse for us.” But the same law that says, “Cursed is every one that continueth not in all things which are written in the book of the law to do them,” hath said, he that doth these things “shall live in them” The law does as much fix obedience as the condition of eternal life, as it does disobedience as the condition of eternal death; one is as much established as the other. Therefore, there is exactly the same need from the law of obedience’s being fulfilled in order to the obtaining the reward, as there is of death’s being borne in order to escape a punishment. There is just the same necessity by the law of perfect obedience preceding life, as there is of death’s following disobedence. The law is without doubt as much an established rule in one case as ‘tis in the other:** [^013-note25] **for surely, if God bestows an infinite reward on a person, he bestows it as a testimony of his acceptance; so that this supposition of God’s giving persons an interest in Christ as a reward of their obedience, if it be scanned, destroys itself: for it supposes that men’s own obedience is sufficient so far to recommend ‘em to God’s favor and acceptance, that God bestows an infinite blessing upon them in reward for it, as great a blessing as salvation itself, yea, a blessing that includes it. So that there can be no need of Christ’s righteousness to recommend a man to God’s favor to that degree as to have an infinite reward; for he has that by his own obedience, antecedent to his interest in Christ’s righteousness. If our own righteousness be sufficient to entitle us to a reward as great as salvation itself, then there is no need of Christ’s righteousness: for we don’t need any thing to entitle us to anything greater than salvation.**

<!-- p.   -->

IMPUTATION. [^013-note26] **And again, the word is not only used in this sense, but ‘tis used in this sense with respect to the righteousness of Christ. There is no more needful in order to prove this, but these two things, viz.: first, that the Apostle says that righteousness is imputed; and second, to prove that when he says righteousness is imputed [Rom. 4:22], he means the righteousness of Christ. This I prove two ways: [first,] it can’t be our own, because saved without works; second, ‘tis certainly the righteousness [of Christ], for ‘tis doubtless that righteousness by which we are made righteous. But the same Apostle tells us that is the righteousness of Christ.**

**Whether it derogates from grace, [“Miscellanies”] no. 191.** [^013-note27]

**’Tis not to put upon a new trial in the same manner as old, but Christ stands instead of the tree of life, [“Miscellanies” no.] 498.** [^013-note28]

**The** [^013-note29] **cause of the futurition not the cause of the thing.**

Christ’s obedience meritorious, for he is not obliged.

To show how a man is altogether hateful. Notwithstanding that faith, which is an holy excellent thing, is antecedent to justification, he is still looked upon as altogether hateful, because nothing is acceptable till he is in Christ and is looked upon [as] righteous in him. Even truly good acts are not acceptable, or, which is the same thing, is not looked upon as good; the good is hid from God. ‘Tis reputed as no goodness.

**Whether they hold any proper merit or no, yet that won’t help out the cause as they long as they maintain that ‘tis our own moral excellency that is what we are justified by.** [^013-note30]

Indeed, ‘tis out of respect to a fitness that by faith a man has to such a state. But that don’t agree that ‘tis from any respect to faith as a

<!-- p.   -->

good or excellent qualification, or from any respect to the excellency there is in faith. There is a twofold fitness to a state.

Obj. **When it is said, “For I was an hungred, and ye gave me meat” [Matt. 25:35], to mention in answer those places, “Her sins, which are many, are forgiven; for she loved much” [Luke 7:47].**

<!-- p.   -->

## Note to Fragment of a Notebook on the Signs of Grace

**The following document is a fragment of a notebook, devoted mainly to the signs of grace and related topics, consisting of five octavo leaves. Together, the fugitive leaves form pages 9-17 of the notebook. The first leaf of the notebook to which these leaves once belonged is apparently the one at Jonathan Edwards College, Yale University.** [^014-note1] **Beyond the content, there are few clues on which to make assumptions about the disassembled notebook. Originally it was of a considerable length, since at one point in the extant leaves there is a reference to “p. 42.” Also, the entries are not deleted with vertical lines, Edwards’ usual manner of indicating he had used a given passage or that it was no longer of interest to him. The handwriting of the leaves dates from the 1740s. We can speculate that this may have been one of a series of notebooks, of which “No. 7” is extant, that Edwards drew up for writing** **Religious Affections** **.**

* * * * *

Fugitive leaves (pp. 9-17) from a notebook on Religious Affections. N.d. [c. 1745-46]. HSP, Dreer Autograph Coll., 126:1, American Clergy, v. III (3 leaves); Etting Collection, 26, p. 36 (1 leaf).

[^014-note1]: **Reproduced in** **Jonathan Edwards Tercentennial Exhibition: Selected Objects from the Yale Collections, 1703-2003** **(New Haven, Jonathan Edwards College, 2003), 20-21.**

## FRAGMENT OF A NOTEBOOK ON THE SIGNS OF GRACE

LIVING ON FRAMES. **Doubting of a thing may arise from unbelief when it comes from a sight of abundance of corruption in the heart. For this may be when persons are far from being in a carnal frame.** JE wrote and then deleted a reference to a passage on the next page, which also is deleted. The passage reads: “There may be the exercise of grace sensibly this way when not very sensible exercise of grace in any other way.”

Faith also is the first exercise of grace by which they must emerge out of a dead frame. They must look to Christ for help from their darkness and deadness and blindness, as the poor blind man. So it is their duty to believe and trust in Christ to be delivered from darkness.

Another evidence that discoveries or a ;scSENSE OF GODS LOVE;rt is not the primary foundation of holy dispositions and affections, or not that wherein grace does fundamentally consist, is that a person may be without this and yet be perfectly holy, yea, in the highest and most exalted and supreme exercises of holiness, as Christ on the cross.

LOVING FOR EXCELLENCY. **If there be any such thing as their being beautiful and lovely in themselves otherwise than as tending to an interest, then it will follow that they may and ought to be loved from that excellency. For if there be such a beauty, then it may be seen as it is; and if it be seen as it is, it will appear beautiful and amiable in itself, as it is, and consequently will be delighted in and loved: for that is implied in its appearing beautiful.**

INFINITE OBLIGATION. **Observe the difference between being infinitely obliged to a thing and being obliged to an infinite thing.**

<!-- p.   -->

PRACTICAL EXERTIONS. **The highest sign is grace, as carried forth into its practical exertions or into the fruit that is produced in practice.**

BEAUTY OF HOLINESS. **See II Chron. 20:21.**

SPIRITUAL TASTE. **For the next two entries, see related arguments in** **Nature of True Virtue** **(** **Works, 9,** **590, 597, and elsewhere?).** **Objection concerning the approbation there is in natural men of virtue, etc.**

In answer, to show that the natural approbation of men’s consciences of virtue, and condemnation of vice, is a thing entirely distinct from the relish that is in a good man of virtue. As appears by this: that at the same time that this testimony of conscience will be in its greatest perfection, viz. at the day of judgment, wicked men will be furthest from that relish; and hatred of holiness and a wicked taste and inclination will be in its greatest perfection.

NATURAL MEN’S LOVE TO VIRTUE. **The love of natural men is as their abhorrence is. A natural man abhors the sight of a rattlesnake; it is an unpleasant sight to him: which is only because he habitually connects it with the idea of hurtfulness to him. So he hates vice no other way, and loves many kind of virtues in the same, when all is originally to be resolved into self-love. There is this, and also the testimony of his conscience concerning the punishableness of vice and rewardableness of virtue. The testimony of a man’s conscience of the punishableness of a thing is a different thing from a sense of the odiousness of it; and the testimony of the conscience of the rewardableness of [virtue] is different from a sense of the beauty {of virtue}. This is manifest in what will be at the day of judgment. And besides, there is a kind of association or habitual connection by education.**

<!-- p.   -->

PRACTICE. **In Ps. 105, last verse, after rehearsing over God’s wondrous works that he had wrought for Israel, this is, in the fine, mentioned as God’s aim in all: “** **That they might observe his statutes, and keep his laws** **.” See p. 42.** The page has not been located.

PRACTICE. **Making Christ the minister of sin and improving free redemption as an encouragement to sin. Jer. 7:9-10, “Will ye steal, murder,” etc. “and come and stand before me in this house, which is called by my name, and say, We are** **delivered** **to do all these abominations?”**

The end of the JUDGMENT of the Last Day will be [to] make persons manifest, not only to the world but to their own CONSCIENCES. Jude 1:14-15, “Behold, the Lord cometh with ten thousands of his saints, to execute judgments upon all, and to convince all that are ungodly among them of all their ungodly deeds which they have ungodly committed, and of all their hard speeches which ungodly sinners have spoken against him.”

EVANGELICAL HUMILIATION. **Is. 46:12, “Hearken to me, ye stouthearted, that are far from righteousness.” Ps. 119:21, “Thou hast rebuked the proud that are cursed, which do err from thy commandments.”**

**True saints are of a MERCIFUL spirit. The word commonly used in the Old Testament for saints is** **Kasidim** **. See Ps. 116:15 and other places in Buxtorf.** **Johann Buxtorf,** **Lexicon Hebraicum et Chaldaicum** **(Basil, 1645). JE’s personal copy is in Princeton University’s Firestone Library; the shelf list indicates that David Brainerd brought the book with him to the Edwards household when he came to stay there during his last illness. See Rice, “JE at Princeton,” p. 87.**

PRACTICE. **He that gives his heart to Christ must needs give his body to him. It would be as absurd to suppose the heart and outward practice, soul and body, to be separated in the true and faithful spouse of Christ, so that she should indeed give her heart to him but serve other lovers with her body, as the heart and body in a temporal spouse, so that she should be indeed true and faithful in her heart and yet give her body to other lovers. Would not**

<!-- p.   -->

**all the world call that man a fool, that should regard the pretenses of his wife that pretended that she was constant and true in her conjugal affections to him, and yet lead the practice of a whore?**

PRACTICE. **Prov. 14:2, “He that walketh in his uprightness feareth the Lord: but he that is perverse in his ways despiseth him.”**

BOLDNESS for CHRIST. **When the angels of God came to Jacob, and he called the name of the place “Mahanaim,” i.e. “two armies,” the host of angels was one army, and Jacob’s train going against Esau and his host of persecuters of the church was the other army, representing the church militant; and as manner of warning against enemies and persecuters, they encounter Esau and his 400 men, not with swords and bloody weapons but with liberal presents, with great kindness, with respect, with love and friendliness and humble respect, and with entreaties, and with prayers and wrestlings with God.**

PRACTICE. HUMILIATION. **Prov. 26:16, “The sluggard is wiser in his own conceit than seven men that can render a reason.”**

How this is true in matters of religion. How there is a sort of religion and pretended high experience and great affections that don’t issue in a diligent and laborious constant practice of religion, but in settled slothfulness and neglect of the difficult parts of Christian practice that yet exceedingly lifts men up with a high conceit of their own goodness and high experience, and causes ‘em to look on themselves vastly better than others, and to look down with contempt on them that are real, humble, modest Christians.

HUMILIATION. SPIRITUAL PRIDE. **Prov. 26:12, “Seest thou a man wise in his own conceit? there is more hope of a fool than of him.”**

<!-- p.   -->

TALKING MUCH AND ZEALOUSLY NO SIGN. **Prov. 26:23, “Burning lips and a wicked heart are like a potsherd covered with silver dross.”**
